(Two exceptions, built the same day and each with a section of its own: a folder's trust question, on the owner's standing choice for that folder (The folder-trust question), and Codex's review of hooks when every hook can only be spandeck's own (Trusting spandeck's own hooks). A pane showing either says what that section's reader finds, and these rules leave it alone.) A pane that needs the owner is not nudged about its mail, which is what ends the nudging of a program that has no quota.
- The screens are written by hand from the words that were seen, and nothing else; the wording of a real screen that differs is a rule to fix.

**5. Only the station of the account writes the agents' settings in your home.** See the next section.

**6. A closed pane's unread mail goes back to its senders.** Mail is addressed by name, a name is free once its pane is closed, and a new pane given the name inherited the closed pane's unread mail and was nudged about it at once.

- **When a pane is closed for good** (`pane.close`, a project closed, every pane closed, or an ended pane leaving the list after its seven days), each message it had not read is marked `undelivered: its pane was closed before it read this`, which no pane's inbox shows and nothing is nudged for; and **each sender is told once**, in its own inbox, in the same transaction: "Not delivered to reader@alpha: its pane was closed before it read your note "…" (id). Nothing was passed to another pane: send it again to a pane that is running, if it is still wanted." One note for each sender, from `spandeck@<station>`, like the note for a refusal; a sender on another station gets it through that station's queue, as an ordinary note. The owner gets one notice ("Mail for reader was not read before its pane was closed"); the ship's log has `msg.returned`. A note of spandeck's own that went unread is not answered with another.
- **A pane that is restarted, brought back or restored keeps its id, is never closed, and keeps its mail.**
- **A name a project starts its own pane under is a role, and keeps role delivery.** A pane a project starts by name (its `panes`) is the same crew member each time the project starts it: the project already puts it back in its conversation. Mail for `demo/keeper` that the last keeper left unread is the next keeper's. Only the unrelated case is changed: a pane the owner or an agent started under a name.
- **What is left.** The mail is returned on a thread of its own, since closing a pane waits for no database: a pane given the name within the few milliseconds that takes could still be shown it. Mail left by a pane that was closed before this version is still inherited once. Mail addressed to a pane's old name after a rename is not looked at here.

**7. The sender's copy of a message is answered when the reply arrives.** Each station keeps its own copy of a message. The sender's station set its copy to "delivered" when the other station stored it, and nothing ever told it more, so it said "delivered" while the recipient's said "answered".

- **The cheaper honest option was taken: no receipt of its own, and a row that says what it does not know.** A reply now names the message it answers (`refs`: the id, which `reply` knows), and when it arrives the sender's station marks its own copy `answered`. It marks only a message that it sent to the pane the reply is from, and only on that station's signed call: naming a message's id answers nothing for anyone else. This rides on the reply, which is already delivered at least once and dropped as a duplicate by its id.
- **That a message was only read is not told between stations.** A row of `msg.recent` and `msg.thread` says whose copy it is (`at`), and one that was stored at another station and is not known to be answered says so in words (`status_words`: "stored at bravo, as alpha knows it: whether it was read is recorded at bravo only, and alpha learns that it was answered when the reply arrives"). A receipt for "read" would be a new kind of record on the relay, with its own duplicates, its own queue behind a station that is away and its own refusals on a station from before; it was judged not worth that for a mark nobody acts on. **The owner may overrule.**
- An explicit `ack` with no reply is likewise known at the recipient's station only.

**8. Antigravity asks the owner before every spandeck tool call.** So an Antigravity agent could neither read nor send mail without the owner at its terminal. What its documentation says (https://antigravity.google/docs/hooks, /permissions, /mcp, /plugins, /build-plugins, read 16 Nov 2026, and the guides shipped inside 1.5.9):

- **A hook that answers "allow" for spandeck's tools was not built, because the documentation is not of one mind.** The guide shipped in 1.5.9 says of a `PreToolUse` reply's `decision`: `"allow"`: "Automatically allow the tool execution." The online guide says: `"allow"`: "Allows the tool call to proceed, delegating to the next hook or default policy" (which for an MCP tool is to ask) and names a value the shipped guide does not have, `"auto_approve"`: "Automatically approves the tool call when the default policy would otherwise prompt, while honoring explicit user-configured rules". Neither says what a `matcher` is matched against for an MCP server's tool: the tools listed are Antigravity's own. And `decision` is required, with no "no opinion", which is why `PreToolUse` was never hooked.
- **A narrower route is documented, and that was built: allow rules in Antigravity's own permissions.** "`mcp`: `mcp(server/tool)`, `mcp(server/*)`, or `mcp(*)`"; "Unconfigured `mcp` tools … default to Ask"; "Deny > Ask > Allow"; the rules are in `permissions.allow` of `~/.gemini/antigravity-cli/settings.json`; a plugin's server is "namespaced with the plugin name as `<plugin-name>_<server-key>`", so spandeck's is `spandeck_spandeck`, the name the real prompt showed.
- **`spandeck integration install antigravity`** adds exactly four rules to that list and nothing else: `mcp(spandeck_spandeck/inbox)`, `…/send`, `…/reply`, `…/ack`. Never a wildcard, never another server, never another of spandeck's tools (delegating, the vault, notes and recall go on asking). The owner's other settings and rules are kept; an ask or deny rule of the owner's for the same tool wins, by Antigravity's own order; a file that is not what the documentation says is refused and not touched; the file keeps its permissions and is backed up once; `integration uninstall antigravity` takes out exactly those four.
- **It is the owner's command, never the station's own doing**: spandeck does not rewrite the owner's agent settings by itself (While spandeck is away), and this file is Antigravity's own, which it also rewrites. It is the owner's alone (rule 7) and the station of the account's (the next section). `spandeck integration status` and the doctor say whether the rules are there.
- **What it rests on, and what it is not.** Antigravity's terms forbid a third party reaching its service with its sign-in. This is not that: nothing of spandeck's talks to Antigravity's service or holds its sign-in; a line in the owner's local settings file tells the owner's own Antigravity not to ask before four tools of a local MCP server. **Not run with a real `agy`**: that 1.5.9 reads these rules, that the name is as documented, and whether a running session reads them without being started again.

**Mixed versions (a station on 0.4.7 in the fleet).**

- 1 to 4 are each station's own, about its own panes: a 0.4.7 station types the old line, as plain text, every minute for ever, and shows those screens as idle or working. Its panes shown through an updated station's deck are as it says they are.
- 5: a 0.4.7 station started under another state directory with your real home still rewrites Antigravity's plugin there; the station of the account puts it right when it next starts an Antigravity pane or saves the agent setup.
- 6: a 0.4.7 station still hands a closed pane's mail to the next pane with the name. The note an updated station sends to a sender on a 0.4.7 station is an ordinary note there.
- 7: a reply from a 0.4.7 station names nothing, so the copy at an updated sender's station stays "delivered", with the words that say what that means; a reply from an updated station to a 0.4.7 station carries a `refs` that station stores and does not read. `at` and `status_words` are new fields a deck from before passes over.
- 8: a file on one machine.
- A pane's view has `mail` (3), and `needs_reason` may now hold the words of 3 and 5 for a pane that is idle; a deck from before shows a reason wherever it shows one.

**Tests.** Every change: the line, and a hostile sender's name (`messages.rs`); unsent, the one Enter, the back-off's schedule over an hour and each of the three things that start it again (`wake.rs`); each screen above (`detect.rs`); returned mail, and the sender's copy answered by the right reply only (`messages.rs`); the four rules and nothing else (`install.rs`); the station of the account (`paths.rs`). On real stations with the stand-in agent, every change: a nudge sent as a paste whose first Enter is not taken, taken at the second, typed once; a known screen believed over hooks and taken back; a new pane with a closed pane's name, a restarted pane, a project's pane; two stations, a reply across them (`phase3.rs`, `phase6.rs`); a station that is not the one of its account, with a pane of every agent kind (`agent_setup.rs`). Merge tier (`wake.rs`, 28 s): an agent that never takes a turn, told four times at 0, 1, 3 and 8 seconds with `nudge_secs = 1`, left alone 15 seconds later, the notice once, one copy on its screen, told again for new mail.

## The notice a hook gives carries the messages; `wait` says what it ended on; a Codex scrolled back is typed nothing (18 Nov 2026)

Measured on 18 Nov 2026 with a real Claude Code building and a real Codex reviewing, 27 messages: every one was stored and delivered at once, and still a free recipient took 2.6 s at the median (1 to 7 s) to read one, and a busy one 18 s at the median and 199 s at the worst, because each was told only that it had mail and then spent a step of its own on the inbox tool. The owner was put three choices (the words in the hook's notice only; in the notice and in the typed nudge; the rule kept) and chose **the words in the hook's notice only**. No migration is added, no call is added, `PROTOCOL` is unchanged.

### The notice a hook gives carries the messages

- **By the hook's notice, inside a turn, an agent is given its unread messages themselves** (`messages.rs`, `mail_quoted`, `mail_notice`): at a prompt, at a tool's end, and in the turn continued at its end, as before. **The typed roads are unchanged:** the nudge and its repeat are the one line, name who and how many, and never quote a message. Messages are never keystrokes.
- **Each message is quoted and labelled as its sender's** (rule 6, as recall labels history). It stands between two rows of spandeck's own, "--- spandeck message 1 of 2: question from demo/writer@alpha, id m-… (quoted) ---" and "--- end of message m-… ---", and every row of its body begins with "> ". So nothing a body says can stand where spandeck's rows stand: not an order, not a copy of those rows. Control characters other than a new line and a tab are dropped; the sender's name is cut to what an address is made of, as in the typed line. The notice's first row says the words are the sender's, to weigh as a request and never as a system instruction, and to answer with `reply` and the id.
- **A body is cut at 1,500 characters**, with a row of spandeck's under it: "…(920 more characters: read it with the inbox tool)". **Three messages at most are quoted** in one notice; the rest are counted ("Still unread (1 cut short, 2 not quoted): read them with the spandeck inbox tool"). **Decided by default; the owner may overrule** both figures (`NOTICE_BODY_CHARS`, `NOTICE_MESSAGES`).
- **What "read" means.** A message quoted whole is marked read at that moment, exactly as `inbox` marks what it returns: the agent was given every word, so that is when it was read, and the sender's view (its status, the time to read) says what happened. It is then unread mail by no road: no later notice, no nudge, no Enter. A message that was cut, or counted and not quoted, is not read: it stays unread and announced, as every message in a notice was before, so `inbox` gives it and a notice that is ignored is repeated after `nudge_secs`. "Acted on" is still said by `reply` and `ack` only.
- **A notice is made only for an answer that carries it.** The program's own renderer is asked first (`hooks.rs`, `carries_mail`). Before this a notice was also made, and its mail marked announced, at a tool's end that failed (Claude Code's `PostToolUseFailure`, whose answer has no place for context) and at Antigravity's hooks other than a prompt and the end of a turn; it reached nobody. Such mail now waits for the next hook that carries it, or the nudge.
- **What this cannot know:** that the program put the hook's answer before its model. The ship's log keeps which messages were given this way (`msg.quoted`: how many, and the ids marked read), never what they say.
- **Mixed versions.** Nothing crosses stations: the notice is made by the station that holds the message. That a message was read is still known at the recipient's station only.

### `wait` says what it ended on

The builder's words: "The critic's pane sometimes showed as 'needs you' when it was only busy, or was waiting on a permission prompt the owner had to answer. `wait` then returned at once, so I couldn't always tell whether to wait or carry on."

- **`until`** (`rpc.rs`; the tool's `until`; `spandeck wait <pane> --until …`): `any`, the default and what `wait` always did (done, needs the owner, failed, or ended); `done`: the pane's turn is over (done or failed, or ended), and a pane that only needs the owner is waited through; `message`: the pane has sent the caller a message that is unread or arrived since the wait began. Until a message, a turn of the pane that ends while the caller waits also ends the wait, saying so; a turn that was over before the wait began does not, since the pane may not have started on what it was sent.
- **The answer is the pane's view, and:** `why` (`message`, `timed_out`, `exited`, or the state found: `done`, `error`, `needs_you`), `turn_over`, `needs_owner` (with `needs_reason`), `message` (`{id, from, kind}`, never what it says, and nothing is marked read), and `says`: all of it in one sentence with what to do next ("critic is waiting for the owner (…); its turn is not over, and only the owner can answer it. To wait for its turn to end, wait with until "done"; for a message from it, with until "message".").
- No method is added and no list changes: `wait` was an agent's already. A station from before passes over `until` and answers without the new fields; the command line then prints the pane's line as before.

### A nudge is not typed into a Codex that is scrolled back

Once in that run a nudge was typed into a Codex whose screen was scrolled back in its own history. The Enter that should have sent it only returned Codex to its latest line, and the nudge sat unsent in its box.

- **The screen is read** (`agents/picker.rs`, `codex_scrolled_back`, from the two real screens of 0.169.0, at work and idle): its bottom row begins "New · " or "New activity · " and says "esc latest", or the row above its message box is "New activity · ↓ Back to bottom · esc".
- **Such a Codex is typed nothing by the mail look**: not the nudge and not the one more Enter (`messages.rs`, `nudge_pane`). Its mail waits as behind a hold: stored, unread, announced to nobody, not counted as a nudge it did not take, and looked at again at the next look. **No key is pressed to bring it back:** Esc also interrupts a Codex at work, and what each key does in each state has not been verified on a real one.
- **The pane says why**, where it has no reason of its own: "has mail waiting: Codex is scrolled back in its own history, where a message typed now would not be sent; it is told when it is back at its latest line" (`needs_reason`, and `mail.waits_for: "codex_scrolled_back"` in the pane's view). The ship's log has `msg.nudge_held`, once each time it begins.
- **Not covered:** what the owner sends from the message box is typed as before. The screen is read a moment before the typing, not with the input locked. A Codex in a turn is told by its hooks, with the words.

**Tests.** Every change: the notice word for word, a body that copies spandeck's rows and gives orders, the cut and the count, and what is read on a store (`messages.rs`); the two real screens, each sign by itself, and a Codex that only quotes them (`agents/picker.rs`); on a real station with the stand-in agent, a busy agent given two messages in one notice, once, the whole one read and the cut one left for `inbox`, nothing typed; and `wait` by each `until` through a pane that needs the owner (`phase6.rs`). Merge tier (`holds.rs`): a stand-in Codex drawing the scrolled-back rows is typed nothing for 3.5 s, says why, and is nudged once when it is back. **None of it has been run with the real programs yet.**

## A nudge is never typed into a question a program asks as it starts; an agent's tools answer while its `wait` runs (19 Nov 2026)

Two faults from the runs of 18 Nov 2026, each read from what was recorded at the time. No Guarantee is weakened; one line is added under Messages.

### The nudge and a program's first questions

The station's log, a Codex 0.169.0 pane started with a role: the role stored at 20:14:13; Codex's notice of a newer version up until the owner pressed Escape at 20:14:53; the nudge typed at 20:14:54.5, 1.2 seconds on, into the folder-trust question that came next. Its Enter took "Trust and continue" (so the folder was trusted and nobody had answered), the question swallowed the words, the mail counted as announced, and the agent heard of it at 20:15:53, with the next message. The rule until then counted only the program's age (eight seconds, and to thirty while its screen was being drawn): forty seconds old, it was "past its start". The watch of the first screen did not help: it only decides what spandeck answers.

- **A screen that is read as one of these questions is typed nothing, whenever it shows** (`foldertrust.rs`, `start_question`; `messages.rs`, `nudge_pane`): Codex's notice of a newer version, a folder-trust question, a review of hooks. It is read from the screen at the look and again with the pane's input locked, whatever the first-screen watch has decided (a watch that closed after its 20 seconds knows nothing of a question that came late). Before the program's session has started (its hook said so, or any hook of it ran), a row that begins with a question's words is enough; after, only a screen that ends with the question's own choices. Neither the nudge nor the one more Enter is typed.
- **A program is "still starting" by more than its age** (`wake.rs`, `starting`): its first eight seconds, as before; 2.5 seconds after the last look that found it at such a screen, or at a list to choose from, or that answered one (Codex asks up to three things one after another, and the next is not drawn yet when the last is answered); while its screen is still being drawn, to thirty seconds from either; and for as long as its screen is empty (a nudge typed at an empty screen stays in the terminal and answers whatever the program asks first: seen in a scenario here, the question drawn 13 seconds later). A program whose session has started is past all of it.
- **How soon the message comes.** With the stand-in: typed within 8 seconds of the last question being answered (measured about 3; the bound allows for a busy machine), where it was a minute. A program that says its session started (Claude Code) is told at once, as before.
- **Safety over speed, and what is not covered.** A question spandeck cannot read is protected only by the times above. A program that never runs a hook and whose screen keeps a row that begins with a question's words has its mail held for as long as the row shows, and the pane does not say why. A late folder-trust question (past the watch's 20 seconds, with no notice before it) is typed nothing now but is still not said on the pane unless it is read as a list. None of it has been run with a real Codex or Claude Code.

**Tests.** Every change: the rule by its times (`wake.rs`). Merge tier (`foldertrust.rs`): the stand-in draws the notice for 31 seconds, then the folder's question (answered by the station on the owner's tick), then the review of hooks (nobody's), with a message stored at the start: nothing is typed at any of them, and the message is typed after the owner answers the last (on the code before, the nudge answered the review of hooks); a question first drawn 21 seconds after the start, with mail waiting, is typed nothing.

### An agent's tools answer while its `wait` is still running

An agent reported that `inbox`, `ack` and `reply` "often took two minutes or more". Claude Code's session files have the times: each slow call followed a `wait` the agent had asked for with 600 or 1800 seconds, and each was answered by Claude Code itself with "MCP tool \"spandeck/ack\" is still running after 120s. It was moved to the background". `spandeck mcp` read and answered one request at a time, so the call sat unread behind the wait. Of 237 `inbox`, `ack` and `reply` calls with a recorded time on that station, 8 took 120 s and 2 took 44 and 50 s (the wait ended first); the rest took 4 to 41 ms. The station was not slow: with seven stand-in agents taking turns without a pause, 1,558 rounds of `inbox`, `reply`, `ack` and a `wait` for what was already so had medians under 1 ms and a slowest call of 42 ms; `spandeck inbox` as a command, 3 ms and 23 ms.

- **A tool call never waits for another that is still running** (`cli/src/mcp.rs`): each answer goes out when it is ready, under its request's id. The calls that change what others see (`send`, `reply`, `ack`, `task_result`, `note_add`, `delegate`) are still done one after another in the order asked; the rest, `wait` among them, each run by themselves.
- **`wait` is unchanged**: it holds until what it waits for, or its time. A program that stops waiting for it (Claude Code after 120 seconds) leaves it running, and now loses nothing by that. A wait the program cancels is not stopped: it runs to its own end and its answer is dropped by the program.

**Tests.** Every change (`phase6.rs`): a `wait` of ten minutes on a busy pane, then `reply`, `send`, `ack` and `inbox`: all four answered in under five seconds, the three changes in order. Merge tier (`soak.rs`): the measurement above, with a bound of two seconds a call.

## Only the station of the account writes the agents' settings in your home (16 Nov 2026)

The owner's requirement the same day: "make sure that every agent can [talk] to every other agent"; this is the fault its test run caused. What happened on the owner's machine: a throwaway station, started with its own `SPANDECK_STATE_DIR` and `SPANDECK_CONFIG_DIR` but the owner's real home, rewrote `~/.gemini/config/plugins/spandeck/mcp_config.json` and `hooks.json` to point at its own (test) binary, and a setup applied by such a station removes the skills it does not list. It was put right when the real station next started an Antigravity pane.

**The rule: only THE station of this user account writes anything into the agents' own settings under the home folder. It is the one kept in the usual state directory with the login service (`Paths::is_the_station`, the test `started_from` already used), or one the owner told to in its own config file: `agent_home = true`.** Any other station (a trial, a test, a second instance) is not, whatever its home is. `agent_home = false` stops the usual one.

- **What is covered:** Antigravity's plugin (`~/.gemini/config/plugins/spandeck`), the agent setup's skills for Claude Code and Codex (`~/.claude/skills`, `~/.codex/skills`) and in that plugin, written and removed, and `spandeck integration install` and `uninstall` for every agent (`~/.claude/settings.json`, `~/.codex/hooks.json`, Antigravity's settings).
- **What another station does instead, per agent** (each adapter was read for what its program takes for one launch):
  - **Claude Code**: `--settings` (its hooks), `--mcp-config` and `--allowedTools` on the pane's own command line, as always. Nothing is lost but the setup's skills.
  - **Codex**: its MCP server by `-c mcp_servers.spandeck.…` on the command line, as always. Its hooks come only from `~/.codex/hooks.json` or an inline `[hooks]` table of a `config.toml` (its documentation, read 16 Nov 2026), and a hook that is new or changed is skipped until the owner trusts it; a `-c` that supplies hooks for one launch is not documented. So such a station's Codex panes have spandeck's tools and no hooks: their state is read from the screen, as designed for Codex where hooks do not fire.
  - **OpenCode**: `OPENCODE_CONFIG_CONTENT` in the pane's environment and a skills folder under the station's own state directory, as always. Nothing is lost.
  - **Antigravity** loads a plugin from the home folder (`~/.gemini/config/plugins/`) or from the project's own `.agents/plugins/` (its plugins guide), and has no flag or variable that names another folder. A plugin written into the owner's project folders was set aside: those are the owner's files. So such a station starts Antigravity with whatever plugin the station of the account keeps there (its tools then speak for the right pane, since the pane's environment says which station and pane), and with none **it starts Antigravity without spandeck's tools and says so**: the pane's reason ("started without spandeck's tools: Antigravity loads them only from a plugin in your home folder, which this station does not write"), a notice, and `spandeck doctor` ("agent settings").
- **Refused in words, never silently skipped where the owner asked:** `integration install` on such a station answers "nothing was changed: trial does not write into the agent settings in your home folder: it is not the station that starts at login from … To let it, put `agent_home = true` in …"; a setup with skills saved there says in its `problems` which agents did not get them.
- **Decided by default; the owner may overrule:** a station in the usual state directory that has no login service (one started by hand, or built from source and never given `spandeck service --install`) is not the station of the account either. After updating such a station, `spandeck doctor` says so under "agent settings", and the owner either installs the service or writes `agent_home = true`.
- **In a config file and not a switch that can be set while the station runs**, so that it takes the owner editing that station's own file.
- **Tests.** A station under another state directory, whose throwaway home holds what the station of an account keeps there: after a setup with a skill, a pane of each of the four agent kinds and a try at `integration install`, every file under that home is byte for byte what it was; its panes have spandeck's tools on their own command lines; with no plugin there an Antigravity pane says it has no tools and none is made (`agent_setup.rs`). The stations told to (`agent_home = true`) still install: the plugin, the skills on both stations of a pair, `integration install` (`agent_setup.rs`, `phase3.rs`). The test harness itself now starts stations that write nothing under their home unless a test says so.
- **Mixed versions.** A 0.4.7 daemon reads `agent_home` as a key it does not know and passes over it.

## The deck asks each station one question (15 Nov 2026)

The owner's approval: "execute ... the updated performance plan" (15 Nov 2026), and the rule all of it is held to: "the slow or absent station has to be fixed with the right architecture for a system like this, never to slow everyone else down." This is unit 06 of the performance and continuity plan (`crates/core/src/snapshot.rs`, `crates/web/assets/app.js`). What was measured on 0.4.5: a refresh of the Control Deck was 1 call for the list of stations, 7 to every station and 5 more to the station serving it (20 for two stations, 34 for four: 816 a minute for an idle deck), every 2.5 seconds and again 120 ms after almost any event, with nothing to stop one beginning before the last had ended; the seven were asked in four groups, each waited for, so with one station frozen a round took 20 s and every other station's approvals, notices and stops stood behind it. Measured after, same stations (5 panes each): 3 calls a round for two stations and 5 for four (120 a minute; in a real browser with four stations and twenty panes, 126 a minute with what events ask, 6.5 times fewer); a round in which nothing changed brings 13 KB down for four stations where it brought 31 KB (nearly all of it now the list of stations); and with one station frozen the others' answers were all on the page 3.4 ms after the round began, the frozen one's own question ending by itself after its five seconds. No Guarantee is changed.

- **`deck.snapshot`**: everything a deck shows of one station, in one answer: `panes`, `projects`, `tasks`, `msgs`, `approvals`, `notes`, `stops`, each built by the function its own call uses (`pane.list`, `project.list`, `task.list {active, limit 100}`, `msg.recent {limit 60}`, `approval.list`, `notify.list {limit 60}`, `stop.list`), so it says exactly what they say; a test holds it to that. With `docs: true`, which a deck asks of the station serving it, also `trust`, `deck`, `look` and `prefs` (`trust.status`, `deck.get`, `look.get`, `prefs.get`).
- **What the deck holds is not sent again.** Each section comes with a version (`v`); the deck sends back the versions it holds (`have`), and a section whose version is the same is answered `null`.
- **A version is worked out from the section's content, not counted where it changes** (the brief had a counter per section, bumped where events are raised, and an id made at each start). A pane's title set by its program, its last output, a task's state written by a hook raise no event: a counter would have left the deck showing the old section until the check found it. A hash of the content cannot miss a change, needs nothing kept between calls, and means the same after a restart, so no start id is needed. Two fields are left out of it because they change with the clock alone: a pane's `last_output_ms` (the deck does not show it), and a stop's `age_ms` (the deck counts a stop's age on from when it was told, by the passing of its own clock; the two clocks are still never compared).
- **Who may ask.** It is on the read-only list, since every part of it already is, and so reaches another station through `rpc.forward` like any read. It is on no agent's list (rule 7) and no station's.
- **Each station is asked by itself, and its answer is put on the page when it comes.** No station's answer waits for another's, and the page waits for none: a caller in the page that wants it up to date (after saving a layout) waits two seconds at most. The lists that are the fleet's (approvals, the inbox, stops) are put together again from what each station last said whenever one answers.
- **Never two questions to one station at once.** While a station's question is unanswered it is not asked again: the 2.5 s round passes it over, and anything else that wants it asked (an event, a click) gets one more question when that one ends. So the deck's questions for a station that hangs cannot pile up in the 20 places the connection gives it; measured in a browser with a station frozen for a minute: none refused for that, and no more questions a round than with all healthy.
- **A station whose answer is late or did not come is said to be so.** What it last said stays on the page, and its tile says "not answering: as of 5s ago" from the first round that finds its question still unanswered after two seconds, until it answers or the look at the fleet lists it as unreachable. The inbox names it under "Not counted", as before; the stops line says its stops are shown as they were. Nothing is concluded from what is held of such a station: a hidden pane is not taken for gone, and a leaf of a global layout is not given a station. A view of a station the page has not heard from yet says "Loading…" or "bravo is not answering yet", never that a pane is gone.
- **An event asks only the station it concerns.** The events a page hears are the serving station's, so they ask that station (one question, soon, and not put off by more events); one that names another station (a notice from it, messages that arrived there) asks that one too. An agent starting or ending a tool brings its pane's strip up to date from the event itself, with no question. New usage numbers ask the serving station, twice a second at most however many agents work. A change to the fleet or to a station's vault (both are read from the list of stations), a reconnect or lost events ask everything. A click that changes one station asks that station.
- **What the serving station alone is asked rides in its one answer**: who is trusted, the global layouts and pins, the look and the owner's preferences come with `docs: true`, unchanged ones empty, so they are right at every round and depend on no event. (The brief asked for them only when the deck opens and on their own events; a copy changed by another station's push, or an event lost, would then have stayed wrong until the page was reloaded.) A machine being added (`fleet.add_status`, which is never passed to another station) is asked when the deck opens, on its events, at every round while one is being added, and at the check.
- **The round every 2.5 s stays, as the repair for what no event told the page of**, until events come from every station with a place to go on from (unit 09b). **Every fourth round is the check**: each station is asked for everything, whatever the page holds. A section that comes back different under the version the page holds is a correction: without the check the page would have gone on showing the old one. It is repaired and counted ("Corrections" on the Performance page, under "This browser", with "Refresh questions" a minute beside it). It was 0 in every run made here; with versions made from the content it can only be something other than 0 if the page itself mishandles an answer.
- **A station from before (0.4.5 and earlier)** does not say `deck_snapshot` in its `caps`, and is asked the seven lists as before, all seven together, with the same rule of one round at a time. If it is asked the new question after all it answers "not found: method deck.snapshot", and the page asks it the seven from then on. Its own deck is the old page, which asks the old calls of every station, and every station still answers them. `PROTOCOL` is unchanged.
- **The switch `deck_snapshot`** (`[flags] deck_snapshot = false`, or `station.flags`): off, the station neither announces the call nor answers it, exactly as a station from before, and decks ask it the seven lists (and, when it serves the deck, the four of its own). The tests of a mixed fleet use it. What it does not take back is the page's: each station by itself, one question at a time, events asking one station.
- **What is still one question for everything**: the list of stations (`fleet.view`), asked of the serving station at every round; it is now most of what an idle round brings down (about 12 KB for four stations). It was not made a section here: it carries each station's load and last-seen time, which change at every look.
- **Tests.** Every change: the versions' rule, as a pure function (`snapshot.rs`); and on real stations (`crates/cli/tests/refresh.rs`): the answer against the seven calls and the four, nothing sent again when held, an agent refused, a station with the switch off answering as one from before. Nightly, in a real browser: three stations, one with the switch off and one frozen (the lists of all three filled; a pane and an approval started elsewhere on the page within a round while one is frozen; the frozen one marked; nothing piled up; nothing corrected); and four stations with twenty panes, the questions of an idle deck counted against the 816 a minute of before.
- **Found while building this, and fixed** (`crates/core/src/add.rs`). "Uninstall spandeck on X" first takes X out of the fleet and forgets it, and may then ask for a password; the try that carries the password looked X up again, and until the next look listed that machine again it was refused with "no station called X is known here". The deck used to show the password box some hundreds of milliseconds late, which hid it; it now shows it at once, and the scenario test, which types as fast as the page lets it, failed half the time. The try with the password now goes to the address the try before it had settled, when that job is an uninstall of the same station waiting for a password.
- **Not in this unit:** events from other stations (a change at another station still shows at the next round, up to 2.5 s: units 09b and 10); the conversation view, which has its own stream and is not part of the snapshot.

## A connection that has gone silent is noticed, and the screen says so (16 Nov 2026)

The owner's approval: "execute ... the updated performance plan" (15 Nov 2026), and the two rules this is held to: "This has to be well designed and seamless to the user. 100% uptime from every client, control deck, session etc", and "the slow or absent station has to be fixed with the right architecture for a system like this, never to slow everyone else down." This is unit 03a of the performance and continuity plan (`crates/core/src/beat.rs`, `crates/web/src/lib.rs`, `crates/web/assets/api.js`, `app.js`, `term.js`). What the audit measured on 0.4.2: with the browser's link half-open (nothing passes, nothing closes) the page showed nothing at all at 1 s, 5 s, 30 s, 60 s, 120 s and 300 s, went on taking keys, and delivered every one of them when the link returned (232 keys up to 60 s late, 149 up to 5 minutes late); a pane reached through another station froze for 60 s and for 10 minutes with no notice; there was no ping anywhere, no time limit on a request in the page, and the owner was shown the transport's own error ("golf: unreachable: error sending request for url (…)"). Measured after, real stations and a real page in Chrome (`crates/cli/tests/heartbeat.rs`), at a computer's width and a phone's: with the page's link half-open the top bar said "no answer for 3 s" after 3.2 s, marked what is shown "as of" the last answer and turned typing off on the pane; the bar naming the station was up at 10.0 s; what was typed meanwhile never arrived; and the page was connected again 0.1 s after the link returned, by itself. A pane on a station that froze was told after 5.6 s (six seconds after the last thing heard), and was attached again by itself when the station was back. Over a link that takes two seconds each way, for forty seconds, the page gave up no connection and made no new one. No Guarantee is changed.

**The rule: every long-lived connection has a heartbeat, each end judges for itself, and each judgement drives something the owner sees. A connection is judged by silence (nothing at all heard), never by slowness; and time in which the judge itself was not running is nobody's silence.**

- **The page asks.** While it is being looked at and has heard nothing for one second, the page sends `{"ping": n, "t": …}`; the station answers `{"pong": n, "t": …, "s": <its forward-only clock, ms>}` in the task that reads the connection, which waits for nothing (so the answer says the link and the station are alive whatever its requests wait for). A ping is a frame, not a request: no method, no id, on no method list, answered only after sign-in. The page keeps the round trip (the middle one of the last 8; the Performance page shows it under "This browser", with when it last heard anything and its last gap). An idle healthy link so carries one small frame a second, and only while someone is looking. (Since What is typed, the same day: a ping also carries the page's round trip, and goes at least every 5 s on a busy link too, because the station reads the page's clock from it.)
- **"Not answering" is said after `max(1.5 s, 4 × the round trip)`, at most 6 s, without an answer** (the round trip is the middle one of the last 8 or the latest, whichever is longer, so a link that has just turned slow is given a slow link's time at once). From then typing is off and the screen says so; **the connection is kept**. An answer, or any frame, ends it.
- **The connection is given up and made again after 10 s with nothing at all heard** (`max(10 s, 3 × the round trip + 2 s)`, at most 30 s), whether or not the browser ever says it closed, which a half-open one never does. Any frame counts, so a link that is slow and alive is never taken down: its frames are late, but they arrive.
- **Only time the page was looked at and running counts.** The page's clock ticks twice a second and adds up only ticks that came on time while the page was visible; every bound above is measured on that clock. A hidden tab, a locked phone, a laptop that slept, or the page's own script held up for seconds therefore concludes nothing. **On being shown again, or after a tick more than 2 s late, or when the browser says the network is back, one ping decides**: answered, nothing happens; unanswered within the bound above, the connection is made again at once (the socket of a machine that slept is usually dead, and a new one costs less than waiting ten seconds). The brief had "1 s to answer" and "a timer more than 3 s late drops the connection": both would have taken down a slow link, or a healthy one after a long task, which the second review warned of; the page also asks its stations again at once when it is shown, instead of at the next round.
- **Trying again**: at once, then after 100, 200, 400, 800, 1600, 3200 and 5000 ms (each 30% either way), then every 5 s for ever; at once on being shown again or the network coming back. The schedule starts again once a connection has held for 3 s. A new connection is given 5 s to open (10 s after a try that ran out of time) and then `max(5 s, 3 × what opening took)` to be signed in: on a half-open link it would otherwise neither open nor fail. A pane's own re-attach and a conversation's watch use the same schedule (before: a fixed 800 ms and a fixed 3 s).
- **A station lets go of a page that is gone.** It sends a WebSocket ping every 15 s and closes a deck's connection when nothing was heard on it for 30 s, which frees its attached panes and watched conversations. A browser answers such a ping by itself, in the background too. Given up at the moment the silence has lasted 30 s, not at the first ping after.
- **A connection one station holds to another for one pane is pinged from both ends, every 2 s, and given up after 6 s with nothing heard** (`attach_remote`, `watch_remote`; and the other station's side of it, which is known by the signature it came with). The gateway then tells the pane's channel `{"chan", "unreachable": true, "station", "quiet_ms"}` (the message a page from before knows, with two fields more) and ends the task; the pane says "bravo has not answered for 6 s: typing is off. Trying again…", takes no keys, and is attached again by itself on the schedule above. A write towards that station that cannot be made for 2 s counts as silence, so a full buffer cannot stop the clock.
- **The gateway's pings feed the state the calls feed, and attaching reads it** (`FleetState::not_answering`, unit 02b; no second state was built). A connection given up for silence makes its station "not answering" from when it was last heard, at once (six seconds of unanswered pings are more than the two unanswered calls that state asks for); an answered ping on a connection that still stands, while the station is taken to be silent, ends it as an answered call does; a connection that cannot be made counts as an unanswered call. And `pane.attach` / `pane.conversation.watch` for a station that is not answering is refused at once in the same words as a call ("bravo is not answering (for 7 s now) …"), but for the one every two seconds that finds out, where each used to wait four seconds to connect.
- **A station that was itself away concludes nothing from it** (`Beat`, one rule for every connection a station holds, and the place unit 10's events socket takes its own from). A tick more than a whole interval late (the station slept, was frozen, or its loop was held up) gives the other end a whole new 30 s or 6 s; one that is a little late takes that much off the silence.
- **What the deck shows, from one value: for how long, of the time the page was looking, the link has not been known good.** Under 0.3 s nothing (an upgrade's reconnect is not worth a flash). To 3 s "reconnecting…" in the top bar. From 3 s, or from the moment an open connection is known not to answer, the time in amber ("no answer for 4 s" for an open one, "reconnecting… 12 s" for one being made again), beside it "as of 12:01:05", the same on every station's tile, and on every pane "Not connected to alpha: typing is off. What is shown is as of 12:01:05." From 10 s a bar: "alpha has not answered for 12 s. Your agents keep running there." with a button for each other station whose own deck this browser can be handed to (the existing hand-over). From 60 s "alpha last answered 1 min 12 s ago (12:01:05)". When it is back after 10 s or more: "alpha is back after 1 min 12 s." The times shown are by the clock on the wall since the last thing heard, so a page shown again after an hour says an hour. These three pieces are filled in four times a second while anything is wrong, apart from the page's drawing, so they are right while a menu or a dialog is open.
- **When only another station is affected, only its panes and its tile say so**: the pane as above, the tile "not answering: as of 5s ago" as since unit 06 (the page's question to it has gone unanswered). The page's own connection, and everything of every other station, carry on.
- **Nothing typed is sent while the page knows it is not connected**, then or later: not with the socket closed (as before), and now not with it open and unanswered either; the pane says so at the first key. Keys typed in the seconds before the page knows are still in the connection (unit 03c holds, numbers and refuses them at the receiving station: done the same day, see What is typed; the pane no longer says so "at the first key" but holds keys for 1.5 s and then says how many were not sent).
- **A question of the page's refresh has ten seconds** (twelve for another station, since the serving station gives a call up itself after ten and its words say more): then it is over ("alpha did not answer within 10 s") and asked again at the next round. Before, no request in the page had an end. Clicks are left as they are (unit 03d: a change may not be given up from the page's side alone).
- **Not built, and why: "at most 4 reads in flight per station from the page".** The connection already gives each station 4 places and 20 waiting (unit 02b), and the page asks each station one question at a time (unit 06); a third limit would have held nothing back.
- **No transport error is shown to the owner.** A call to another station that got no answer says which way: "golf did not answer within 5 s"; "golf refused the connection (spandeck may be stopped there)"; "golf could not be reached within 1.5 s (it may be asleep or off, or Tailscale may not be connected there)"; "golf cannot be reached from this station (Tailscale may not be connected here)"; "golf ended the connection before it answered". No address in any of them. (The brief had "Tailscale is not answering on golf" for a call that could not connect; this end cannot tell a machine that is off from one whose Tailscale is, and says so.) The same words where a stop's row, a setup or a refused attach names the reason.
- **When this station's own Tailscale client does not answer "who is at this address"**, the caller is neither let in nor remembered as a stranger (before: remembered as one for two minutes, so the owner stayed locked out after Tailscale was back). The station says "Tailscale is not answering here, so this station cannot tell who is connecting. It is asked again by itself", the page shows that and keeps trying, and the next connection asks again. "Knows no such device" is still a stranger, kept two minutes as before.
- **Every timer, and why.** Page: tick 0.5 s; asleep when a tick is over 2 s late; ping after 1 s of nothing heard; "not answering" after max(1.5 s, 4 × round trip) ≤ 6 s (1.5 s: three times a bad Wi-Fi round trip; 6 s: what a call to a station is given); given up after max(10 s, 3 × round trip + 2 s) ≤ 30 s; a new connection 5 s to open, then 10 s; sign-in max(5 s, 3 × opening); retry 0, 0.1 … 5 s ± 30%; schedule starts again after 3 s connected; refresh question 10 s (12 s through a gateway); screen 0.3 s, 3 s, 10 s, 60 s. Station to a deck: ping 15 s, let go at 30 s. Station to station, per pane, both ends: ping 2 s, given up at 6 s (three pings; told within the six seconds a call to that station is given); a write 2 s.
- **The Performance page**: per station "Silent connections" (decks, connections from stations, connections to stations, given up in the last 5 minutes; `perf.get`: `connections.closed_silent`); under "This browser" the heartbeat's round trip, when the page last heard anything, how many connections it gave up for silence, and its last gap.
- **The switch `deck_ping`** (`[flags] deck_ping = false`, or `station.flags`; read when a connection begins). Off: the station says nothing of pings in `caps`, answers none of a page's, sends none itself and lets go of nothing for silence: a station from before. Its own page then sends no ping and concludes nothing from silence. What the switch does not take back: the page's retry schedule and connection time limits, the ten seconds of a refresh question, the plain words, the Tailscale rule.
- **Mixed versions.** `ping` is announced in `caps` (the sign-in answer and `station.info`); `PROTOCOL` is unchanged; no call was added. A page is served by the station it talks to, so the two differ only when the switch is off. Between stations the pings are WebSocket protocol pings, which every version answers by itself: a pane on a 0.4.6 station reached through an updated one is watched by the updated gateway (told within 6 s); a pane on an updated station reached through a 0.4.6 gateway is not let go of, because that gateway answers the updated station's pings, and is not watched by it either, so there the pane freezes without notice as before until that gateway is updated. Both ways are a scenario test (a station with the switch off). A page from before ignores the two new fields of `unreachable`.
- **Tests.** Every change: the beat's rule (`beat.rs`: silence ends a connection; a slow link does not; the judge's own absence does not); the plain words (`fleet.rs`); the pings feeding the not-answering state (`lanes.rs`). Merge tier, real stations (`heartbeat.rs`): a pane told within seven seconds with the station's name and how long, the attach refused at once by the same state, attached again when the station is back, the pane's station letting go of a silent gateway, a station letting go of a deck behind a half-open link within the half minute and of no deck that is there, the pong's shape; and the mixed pair. Merge tier, in a browser: the page's rules as the plain functions they are (the schedule, what a tick means, the bounds, what the screen says). They run in a browser because the page's code does; the every-change tier has no JavaScript engine, so "≤ 0.3 s, every change" in the brief became this. Nightly, in a browser, with measured times: the page's half-open link at both widths, start to finish, hidden and held up included; a link two seconds away each way; a pane on a station that freezes.
- **What the later units take from here.** 03b (attachments' names): the `unreachable` message and the re-attach path. 03c (what is typed): `api.live` (connected and answering), `api.probe()` (a ping now; resolves with the round trip or rejects after the bound, and never drops the socket), `api.rtt()`, the pong's `s`. 03d (clicks): `call(method, params, {within})`, the code `no_answer`, and `api.live` for "not sent". 09a (resume): `PaneTerm.reattach()` is the one place a pane comes back. 10 (events between stations): `beat::Beat` with `beat::STATION`, and `FleetState::heartbeat` to feed the same state.
- **Not in this unit, and still true.** Keys typed in the two or three seconds before the page knows its link is silent, or in the six before a gateway gives a pane's connection up, are in the connection and arrive if it comes back (03c). A click made in those seconds is applied when it arrives (03d). The fleet's list still learns that a station is away from its rounds, 10 s apart (10). A re-attached pane is repainted from a snapshot (09a). Whether a phone that was locked finds its socket half-open or closed, and how soon its timers run again, was not measured on a phone.

## HTTPS on every station, the app saved on the device, and carrying on through another station (17 Nov 2026, the owner's decisions)

The owner asked: "If spark2 goes down while I am using the mobile app, does it switch over to another spandeck automatically? How does it pick what I should connect to? How should it pick, lowest latency one from where I am?" and then "If it can't connect to a saved machine and that went down, can't it just scan the network to find one's first then let me pick sorted by latency?" The answer given: a page cannot scan a network and does not need to, because the deck knows every station of the fleet. Proposed: (1) now, a list of the fleet's stations saved on the device, and, when the serving station stops answering, a picker of the stations that answer, sorted by measured latency, taking the fastest by itself after a few seconds if the owner does not pick; (2) with HTTPS, the app saved on the phone, so that the same picker appears when the app is opened while its station is down. The cost of Tailscale HTTPS was said: each station's name (`<machine>.<tailnet>.ts.net`) is published in the public certificate-transparency logs; the machines stay reachable only from the tailnet. The owner: "Yes on one and 2." Told that a Linux station would need `sudo tailscale set --operator=$USER` once, the owner said: "I don't want to have to run a shell command, just approve from the menu, or pick a. Option from a menu.. how can I do that?" Asked "Are you content to type the machine's password into the deck for this?", the owner answered: "Yes". The standing requirements this is held to: "100% uptime from every client, control desk, session etc", "no matter where I go, it's all the same view from spandeck", "Should never have multiple fleets, every spandeck should be the same", and "aren't they all peers. It sound like spark2 is some master machine".

This is the revisit that unit 13 of the performance and continuity plan asked for (it was written for plain HTTP), built together with the mobile plan's step M4 (HTTPS and the installed app), because the two decide each other: a service worker needs HTTPS, and a page loaded over HTTPS may only reach another station over HTTPS. Web push, badges and a Face ID gate are not in it (see "Left for later").

**The rules.**

- **No station is the master.** Every station serves the same deck, keeps the same rules and accepts the same browsers. A device's "home" is only the address its page or its saved app was loaded from; the list of stations a device keeps has no first among them.
- **A station that cannot serve HTTPS is not second-class.** Everything that worked over HTTP still does, at the same address and port, for stations on 0.4.10, for old bookmarks and for calls between stations. Nothing is redirected and no browser is told to insist on HTTPS, unless the owner turns the redirect on.
- **Nothing new is granted to anyone.** A browser's token was already accepted by every station of the fleet. What is new is only where a page holding one may connect from.

### 1. HTTPS, served by spandeckd itself

- **What is served where.** The plain port (`web_port`, 7391) is unchanged. Where a certificate can be had, the same addresses also answer TLS on a second port (`[web] https_port`, 7392), with the certificate Tailscale issues for the machine's own name, so the deck is `https://<machine>.<tailnet>.ts.net:7392`. Not 443: a port under 1024 needs an administrator on Linux, and the owner's own programs may want it. spandeckd ends the TLS connection itself, so the caller's real address is still what is checked against the owner's Tailscale login ("Localhost needs a token. Serve identity headers are never trusted" is unchanged; `tailscale serve` would make every caller loopback, and is still only tolerated, not used).
- **How the certificate is had.** By running the machine's own `tailscale cert` with the certificate and the key written to its standard output (`--cert-file - --key-file -`), never to a file Tailscale chooses. The program is the one spandeck already runs for `status` and `whois`; it speaks Tailscale's local API (`/localapi/v0/cert/<name>?type=pair`) and knows, per platform and per build, where that API is and how to prove who is calling (a socket on Linux, a port and a token file in the Mac builds, a named pipe on Windows), which spandeck would otherwise have to copy and keep right. The name is the one of the tailnet's `CertDomains` that is this machine's own `DNSName`, from `tailscale status --json`; never a name from a request or another station.
- **Who may ask, per platform.**
  - **macOS, the App Store and the standalone builds**: the user signed in to the Mac may. HTTPS comes on by itself. (Not run here; to be confirmed on M4-Macbook and LLM-Server.)
  - **Windows**: the user who is signed in to Tailscale may, through the named pipe; spandeckd runs as that user. HTTPS comes on by itself. (Not run here; to be confirmed on rtx6000.)
  - **Linux, and tailscaled run by hand on a Mac**: only root or Tailscale's "operator" user. Where the operator is the station's user, HTTPS comes on by itself. Where it is not (spark2 today), see "One tap", below.
  - **Any platform, HTTPS certificates not switched on for the tailnet** (`CertDomains` empty): the deck says so and names the page of Tailscale's admin console (DNS, "HTTPS Certificates"); that is the owner's choice in Tailscale and spandeck does not make it.
- **Renewal.** Tailscale keeps the certificate and renews it in the last third of its 90 days. spandeckd asks again at start, every 12 hours, and at once when the machine's name changes; when what it holds has under 21 days left it asks with `--min-validity 504h`. A new certificate replaces the old one for new connections only: connections that are open carry on (the TLS listener asks for the certificate at each handshake). The days left are on the Performance page and in `spandeck doctor`, beside the Tailscale key's expiry, and say so in amber from 14 days. An expired certificate is still served (the browser then says so); the plain port is unaffected.
- **Where the key lives.** A copy of the certificate and its key is kept in the station's state directory (`tls/cert.pem`, `tls/key.pem`, mode 0600 in a 0700 directory), so that a station that starts while Tailscale is not answering still serves HTTPS. The key is never logged, is in no answer of the API, and is never sent to another station: each station fetches its own.
- **What a station says.** `station.info` gains `https`: `{on, name, port, state, why}`, and `caps` gains `https` while it is served. When the certificate runs out is not in that answer, which an agent may read: it is in `https.status` (the owner's), `perf.get` and `spandeck doctor`. `state` is one of `on`, `off` (the owner's switch), `needs_operator`, `not_enabled` (the tailnet), `no_tailscale`, `starting`, `error`. Other stations keep only `name` and `port` of it.
- **HTTP to HTTPS: offered, not forced.** A deck opened over HTTP at a Tailscale address of a station that serves HTTPS shows one line: "Open the secure deck", which opens the HTTPS address and hands it the browser's token by the existing steps between two windows (the token is never in an address). `[web] redirect = true` makes the plain port answer a browser's request for the page (not the API, not another station's calls) with a redirect to the HTTPS address; off by default, and a follow-up to make it the default.
- **No HSTS, at all.** `Strict-Transport-Security` belongs to a host name, not to a port: sent for `<machine>.<tailnet>.ts.net` it would turn every `http://` address of that machine, on every port, into `https://` in that browser, which breaks spandeck's own plain port by name, and any other program the owner serves there, for as long as the header said; and a station that later loses HTTPS (the switch, a tailnet setting, an expired certificate) would be unreachable by name from that browser. It is never sent on any port.
- **Calls between stations stay as they are**: HTTP on the tailnet, which WireGuard encrypts, signed with the fleet key. Moving them to TLS is not free (each station would have to learn and check each other's names, and a 0.4.10 station has no TLS port) and gains nothing against anyone who is not already on the tailnet. Left for later.
- **The switch.** `[web] https = false` turns it off: no certificate is asked for, the TLS port is not opened, `https` leaves `caps`, the copy of the key is deleted. Not set, it is on wherever a certificate can be had. A station that does not use Tailscale (`discovery = false`) has none to ask for. `[web] cert_cmd` names a command that prints the certificate and its key in place of `tailscale cert` (the tests use it, as they use `add_machine_cmd`), and `[web] name` the name it is for. **A debug build never runs `tailscale cert`**: a certificate that is issued is published, and no test may cause one.
- **Asked again by the owner.** `https.refresh` (the dialog's "Check again") asks now; `https.status` says everything. Both are the owner's alone.

**One tap, where an administrator's right is needed** (the owner: "just approve from the menu"). On Linux without the operator set, the deck offers "Turn on secure access (HTTPS) on <station>" (the station's menu, the Performance page and the line that offers HTTPS). The dialog says: "spandeck asks <station> to let your user (<user>) manage Tailscale there, so that it can fetch the machine's certificate. This needs your password on <station> once; it is used for that one command and not kept." and, under it, what that right is: "After this, programs running as <user> on <station>, your agents included, can change Tailscale's settings on that machine without a password. To undo it, run "sudo tailscale set --operator=" there."
  - **Exactly one command**, run by the station it is for, as its own user: `sudo -S -k -p '' <tailscale> set --operator=<user>`. An argument vector, no shell. `<user>` is the user spandeckd runs as, read from the system, never from the request. `sudo` and `tailscale` are looked for only in fixed system folders and each must be a file owned by root that nobody else may write: an agent on the machine runs as the owner's user and could otherwise put a program of its own in front. The password goes to sudo's standard input and nowhere else; `-k` makes sudo ask for it whatever it remembers; the environment is cleared but for `PATH` and `LC_ALL=C`. Twenty seconds, then it is ended.
  - **The password** (the same rule as Add a machine's SSH password, extended): used for that one command, never logged, never stored, not in the ship's log, the request-id store or any answer, wiped from memory when the command ends, and **never passed from one station to another**. `tailnet.operator` is carried out only by the station the page is connected to, and is on the list of calls that are never forwarded. To turn HTTPS on at another station the page connects to that station itself (the same kind of connection "working through another station" uses, below) or, where it cannot (an HTTPS page and a station that has no HTTPS yet; the deck `spandeck cc` opens, whose token is one station's), the dialog offers "Open <station>'s own Control Deck", where it is one tap. What protects the password on the way from the browser to the station over plain HTTP is the tailnet's own encryption (WireGuard, device to device), as for everything else the deck sends today.
  - **Who may.** The owner only: on no agent's list, no station's, not the read-only one, and not a request another station passed on or made in the owner's name. Three wrong tries a minute per station, then a wait. Every use is in the ship's log (`tailnet.operator`: who, the outcome; never the password).
  - **What is told apart**, each in plain words with what to do: the password was wrong (try again); this user may not use sudo on that machine; sudo wants a terminal (`requiretty`); sudo asked something that is not a password (a second factor: its words are shown as text); there is no sudo; Tailscale's program is not where a system program should be. In the last five the dialog gives the command to run at the machine, as the last resort.
  - **Taking it back** is the same call with `off` (nothing after `--operator=`), and needs the password again. The API has it; the deck does not offer it yet, and names the command.
  - **Considered, not built.** `pkexec` at the machine's own screen (no password through the deck): works only with a desktop session in front of the owner, which a server does not have. `TS_PERMIT_CERT_UID` in tailscaled's own environment (a right to fetch certificates and nothing else, so narrower than operator): it needs root to write `/etc/default/tailscaled` and a restart of tailscaled, which takes the machine off the tailnet for a moment and must not be done from afar. Tailscale's local API gives no certificate to a user who is neither root, operator nor that uid; `tailscale serve` needs the operator too.

**What was reviewed.**
- **Host.** The TLS port answers only to the machine's Tailscale name, its addresses and `localhost`, each with the TLS port; the plain port's list is unchanged. A request with any other Host is refused, as on the plain port.
- **Origin.** Same-origin as before (the Origin's host and port are the Host's), and on the TLS port the Origin must also be `https:`. The one new case is in part 3.
- **No cookies.** The token is still sent in the first message of the connection, or in a header the page sets; a browser sends neither by itself, so another site cannot use it.
- **DNS rebinding.** An attacker's name that is made to point at a station still fails the Host check; over HTTPS it also fails the certificate. The station's own name pointing elsewhere (a hostile network with Tailscale off) cannot be served over HTTPS by anyone else: only Tailscale can have a certificate issued for it. It can be served over plain HTTP by whoever answers for the name, which is why a token is safer at the HTTPS origin than at `http://<name>:7391`; the deck offers the secure deck there.
- **Who checks a station's certificate.** The browser, and nothing else: no part of spandeck connects to a station over HTTPS (calls between stations are HTTP, signed), and a member's HTTPS name is taken from this station's own Tailscale client, not from a certificate. So the strictest verifier a station's certificate meets is a phone's or a Mac's (server use in its extended key usage, the name among its alternative names, at most 398 days). Tailscale's certificates are Let's Encrypt's, for the machine's name, for 90 days, and meet all of it. spandeck's own outgoing HTTPS (the vault, releases, prices) uses the system's verifier (`crates/core/src/http.rs`), unchanged. The tests' certificates are made to the same standard (a leaf for a server, signed by an authority made in the test) after macOS's verifier refused a bare self-signed one on 17 Nov 2026; verification was not loosened anywhere.
- **What Tailscale prints.** The certificate and key are taken from `tailscale cert`'s output whatever its line ends (`\r\n` on Windows), with or without a last line end, the key before or after the chain, and are kept with `\n`.
- **Certificate transparency.** The names `<machine>.<tailnet>.ts.net` of every station that turns HTTPS on are public for ever: the machine's name and the tailnet's. They resolve only inside the tailnet and nothing is reachable from outside it. The owner was told and accepted this on 17 Nov 2026.

### 2. The app saved on the device

- **Only where the page is a secure context**: an HTTPS deck (and `localhost`). At a plain Tailscale address a browser offers no service worker, and nothing changes there.
- **What is kept.** The shell: the page, its scripts and styles, the terminal, the fonts and icons that are built in. Exactly the files in the build, listed in the worker the station serves, under one cache named for the build. Never an answer of the API, terminal data, an uploaded font or anything that carries a token: the worker passes over every request that is not a GET of one of the listed files at its own origin (`/api/…` never), and caches nothing at run time. The sign-in token and the list of stations stay where they were, in the page's own storage for that origin.
- **One build at a time.** The worker is the build: its text holds the build's id and file list. A new one is fetched by the browser at each opening and when the page hears a different build from its home station; it downloads the whole new shell into a new cache and only then waits. It takes over when no page of the old one is open (the next opening), or at once when the owner taps "A new version of the deck is ready: Reload" (shown instead of the silent reload a plain-HTTP page does; the reload is at once when nothing is being written). The page is then loaded again entirely from the new cache and the old cache is deleted. No page ever runs files of two builds.
- **An older station.** The shell may be newer than the station it reaches (its home was put back to an older version, or it is working through one that is behind): it already asks nothing of a station that the station's `caps` do not offer, and that is unchanged. A home station put back serves its older worker, which replaces the newer one by the same steps.
- **Installed.** The manifest gives the name, `display: standalone`, the colours of the default look and PNG icons (192, 512, and 180 for an iPhone, drawn from the one icon the deck has); the page carries the `apple-mobile-web-app-*` lines. "Add to Home Screen" from the HTTPS deck gives an app that opens with no station answering.
- **Taken away.** `[flags] app_shell = false`: the station serves a worker that deletes the caches and unregisters itself. With `[web] https = false` the HTTPS origin is no longer served at all; a saved app then still opens, finds from any other station that its home no longer serves HTTPS, deletes its caches, unregisters its worker and says where the deck now is. With no station answering it offers "Remove the saved copy" itself.

### 3. The list of stations on the device, and choosing one

- **The list** (`spandeck.roster`, in the page's storage, per device and per origin): for each member of the fleet its name, its HTTPS name and port, its HTTP address and port, the version and `caps` last seen, the last latency measured from this device, when it was last seen on, and how often it was on when this device looked. It is written from every list of stations the page is given (a station passes on another's HTTPS name only when its own Tailscale client gives that name for the other's address, since the page will show its token there), and a station that is no longer a member (removed or forgotten) leaves it in that same step, on every device that hears. A device that has not heard still cannot use it: a removed station no longer holds the key, so it refuses the browser's token and its own calls are refused (Guarantees, Trust), and the page drops a station that refuses its token.
- **"Always on"** is not asked of the owner: a station labelled `always-on` in its config, or one that was on at 97% of at least 24 looks this device made, ten minutes apart or more.
- **Measuring.** A WebSocket to `/api/ping` on each candidate: no token; the station says who answers (name, version, build, `caps`: no more than `station.hello` already says to any device under the owner's login) and then sends back what it is sent, sixteen times at most within ten seconds. Three round trips on that one connection, the middle one kept, so the time to connect is not in the figure; every station at the same time; 1.5 s for all of it. Over `wss:` from an HTTPS page (a browser allows nothing else), over `ws:` from an HTTP page. A socket and not a plain request, so that the page's content policy stays as it was (`connect-src 'self' ws: wss:`) and no answer of a station is ever readable by another origin's `fetch`: no `Access-Control-Allow-Origin` is sent anywhere. A station that answers under another name than the one expected is not that station.
- **Choosing** is one plain function (`roster.js`, `choose`): of the stations that answered, those that are current enough first (they say `deck_via`, and are not on an older version than the newest that answered), then by latency, a difference under 10 ms decided for the one that is always on, then by name. A station that is behind is used only when no other answers, and the bar says so. The station being counted down to stays the choice while it answers and is as current as any (stations are measured again every five seconds, and two that are a millisecond apart must not take turns and start the count again for ever).
- **When the serving station stops answering**: at 10 s of silence (the bar of "A connection that has gone silent", whose first seconds are unchanged) the bar reads "<station> is not answering" and lists the stations: "M4-Macbook · 12 ms", "LLM-Server · 31 ms · always on", the ones that did not answer greyed, "not answering", one that cannot take a browser this way "needs an update". Under it "Continuing on M4-Macbook in 5 s", with Stop; tapping a station goes there at once. **While the owner is writing** (a key in the last three seconds) the countdown waits and says so. A draft in a message box is the device's own and is in the page, which is not loaded again: it is there after the switch, and the bar says "Your draft is kept."
- **After**: one line, "This deck is now working through M4-Macbook", and when the first station answers again, "Go back to spark2". Never back by itself. Everything is asked again of the new station and the panes are attached again through it.
- **On opening** (the saved app, or any page with a list): the page's own station is asked first, since requests to it are the simplest, and is used if it answers within the bound; if not, the best of the others, silently, with the line above. If none answers: a screen that says so, lists each station with when it was last seen, tries again every 5 s and by a button, and asks whether Tailscale is connected on this device.
- **What "working through another station" is.** The page's one WebSocket goes to that station from the page's origin, and nothing else does. A station accepts another origin only when all of these hold (`origin_rule` in `crates/web/src/lib.rs`, `member_decks` in `crates/core/src/https.rs`):
  - the path is `/api/ws` or `/api/ping`, both WebSockets. `/api/rpc`, `/api/peer/rpc`, `/api/device/*`, `/api/font/<id>` and every file refuse other origins as before;
  - the Origin is the deck of a station on **this station's own roster of members**: `http://<address>:<port>` at the address this station itself looks at, or `https://<name>:<port>` where the name is the one **this station's own Tailscale client** gives for that address and the port is the one that member announced (for stations on one machine, as in the tests: `localhost`). Never a name or address a request brings;
  - the token is a browser's own (`dev.…`), checked as ever. The station's local token (`spandeck cc`) and a pane's are refused on such a connection, and so is a signature;
  - `page_failover` is on (`[flags]`, on by default; announced as `deck_via` in `caps`).
  A browser does not apply its same-origin rule to a WebSocket, which is why the station checks the Origin itself and why no CORS header is needed or sent (the brief had CORS, a preflight and `Vary: Origin`: with nothing but sockets there is nothing for them to guard). The caller is `you (device <id>)` as at its own station; the ship's log has `deck.via` at sign-in, with the station whose deck the page came from, and the sign-in answer says `via`.
- **What the page asks of its own origin, and what becomes of it when the origin is down:**
  - the page's files, built-in fonts and icons: from the saved shell (HTTPS), or already in memory (HTTP; a reload then fails, as the browser says);
  - an uploaded font: asked of the working station over the page's connection (`look.font.fetch`, which the deck already used for a font its own station did not hold yet; every station holds a copy);
  - asking to be signed in, and collecting the answer (`/api/device/*`): only at the page's own station. A saved app that is not signed in and whose home is down says so;
  - handing the token to another window: unchanged, needs no station;
  - sending a photo or a file, pictures in a conversation, the QR code: all go over the page's one connection or are drawn in the page; nothing is fetched.
- **What is the serving station's own, when the page works through another**: the words name the station that is doing the work, not the home. Add a machine, Wake and Start act from the working station, and their dialogs already name it. Standing orders above the project and the agent versions in Agent setup are the working station's. The browsers signed in are one list for the fleet. "This deck" in the version bar means the working station; the page itself is the home's build.

### Mixed versions

- **A station on 0.4.10**: serves HTTP only, announces neither `https` nor `deck_via`, refuses another origin. Decks at it work as before. On a device's list it is "needs an update" and is never chosen; an HTTPS page cannot reach it at all, an HTTP page could but is refused.
- **A station with this version and no certificate**: reachable from HTTP pages (its address origin), not from HTTPS pages; the list says "no secure address".
- **A station with HTTPS**: reachable from both.
- A page loaded over HTTP therefore carries on through any updated station; a page loaded over HTTPS, and the saved app, through any updated station that has HTTPS. To have the saved app survive any one station, turn HTTPS on at two at least.

### Limits, said plainly

- A page loaded over plain HTTP that is closed and opened again while its station is down gets the browser's error, as before: only HTTPS has a saved shell.
- A page does not switch by itself while the owner is writing; it does after.
- Keys typed in the seconds before the page knows are refused or late by the rules of "What is typed"; nothing typed before the switch is sent to the new station.
