"If it can't download from spark2 it should install the binary from git" (the download, from GitHub, had worked; the dialog did not say so). And the same day: "What does spark2 have to do with adding this machine, aren't they all peers. It sound like spark2 is some master machine".

- **The stations of a fleet are peers, and the words say so.** A new machine joins "your fleet", never a station. The station that does the adding is the one the page is open on, and where it is named the reason is given with it: "spark2 (the station this page is open on) connects to it". Any station can add a machine. What stays with that one station by design: the SSH connection and the password, which never pass between stations, and the switch that allows adding, which is per station.
- **The same in what a join says elsewhere.** The notice reads "This station joined the fleet spark2 is in" (before: "joined spark2's fleet"); Access and `spandeck fleet --join` say the request is approved "at any station of that fleet (spark2 was the one asked)". What is not only wording: a request is held by the station that was asked, which hands over the key, so an answer given at another station is passed to it, and Access says so.
- **A join looks for the station it is to ask.** `fleet.join` (and `spandeck fleet --join`) on a station that does not see the station named asks every machine under the owner's login now and again each second, for 20 seconds at most, and only then fails, saying what it tried: that Tailscale did not answer on that machine, or how many machines were asked and which stations answered. Before, it failed at once, and a station that had only just started had seen nobody. A second join within 90 seconds of one that looked that long looks once and does not wait again.
- **A station can be named by its address** (`spandeck fleet --join 100.x.y.z:port`, or the address alone): only an address under `peers` in that station's config, or a Tailscale address of a device under the owner's login. It is asked directly, with no look.
- **A station of the same fleet may be asked instead.** When the station named is listed and does not answer, a station that answers and says it is in the same fleet (`station.hello`) is asked. Nothing is taken on that word but whom to ask: the request still shows a code and is approved by the owner, at any station of that fleet. A station that is not in this fleet and has stopped answering leaves the list, so this only helps while it is still listed; the script below does not depend on it.
- **The script asks each station of the fleet, and asks again on a release that gives up at once.** It is given the station that sends it and up to three other stations of the fleet that answer, by name and, where it is a Tailscale address, by address. It asks each by name in turn. After "no station called X was found" (what every release to 0.4.10 says, at once) it runs `spandeck fleet`, which every release has and which looks at the tailnet and waits for the look, and goes through the names again, three times at most, 2 and 4 seconds apart. Then it asks each by address, which a release from this one on takes and an older one answers as it did a name. The log says what it is doing ("Asking to join the fleet (looking for its stations: spark2, m4)").
- **When the asking fails the dialog says what worked first**, in three pieces: "spandeck 0.4.10 was installed on monitoring and is running." (the machine says its version itself, `spandeck --version`); what did not, in plain words with what that machine said; and what to do: "Try again", or on that machine `spandeck fleet --join <any station of your fleet>`, with the fleet's stations named. The same three for a request that was refused or ran out of time.
- **"Try again" after that only asks again** (`fleet.add` with `again`). It connects to the address the try before it used and has spandeck there ask; nothing is downloaded or installed a second time. Before, it ran the whole install again, or, once the station doing the adding had seen spandeck running there, refused the machine as "already a station". It is offered only when the try before installed and started spandeck and failed after that; if spandeck no longer answers there, Add a machine installs it again.
- **The dialog.** Under the user name: "If monitoring asks for a password you will be asked for it next; it is used for that one connection and not kept." A machine that is picked becomes one row with "Change", so the row and the box are both in sight above a phone's keyboard. The switch is no longer a button beside "Connect and install", where "Turn adding machines off on spark2" read as turning spark2 off: it is a quiet last line, "Stop spark2 from adding machines", with what it does (only this feature; the station and its panes keep running) and that the dialog offers to turn it on again. One line says who connects and what to do if that station is down: open the deck on another station. On a phone "Add a machine…" is also in the top bar's menu, so it is reached from Recent as well as from By station.
- **Mixed versions.** A station on 0.4.10 or older that is being added: the script's retries and its list of names are what make it join; it takes no address. A station on 0.4.10 doing the adding sends the old script: one try, one name; a new release on the machine added then looks for 20 seconds by itself. The Control Deck is the adding station's own, so the words and "Try again" are those of that station's version.

### A dialog fits what is in sight (17 Nov 2026, the owner's report)

"I can't even see what I am typing for username": the box was behind the iPhone's keyboard. Since 0.4.10 the page (`#app`) is what is in sight, the visual viewport; dialogs were not.

- Every dialog and sheet is placed where the visual viewport is and is never taller than it: the backdrop from `--vv-top` and `--vv-height`, which the page keeps as it keeps `#app`. The dialog's head stays; **the dialog's body is what scrolls**, on a computer too (before, the backdrop scrolled and took the head with it). The page itself never scrolls.
- With the keyboard up the dialog takes all the room there is, and the box being typed in, with its label and the line under it, is kept in sight by scrolling the dialog's body, by as little as shows it. Nothing calls `scrollIntoView`.
- Toasts stand above the keyboard.
- The sign-in page for a new browser has no box to type in and is unchanged.

### A machine that cannot download is sent the release over SSH (17 Nov 2026, the owner's decision)

While adding a Mac from the phone the owner said: "if it can't download from spark2 it should install the binary from git". Installing from GitHub is what already happened (the machine downloads the release itself; nothing comes from spark2). The gap is the opposite one: a machine that cannot reach GitHub. Until now the script probed the release page (`curl -I <releases>/latest`, 20 seconds) before installing and ended with "could not reach GitHub"; a download that broke off afterwards ended as "the install script failed". Asked whether the station that does the adding should send the program over the SSH connection it already has: "Yes I want it".

- **When.** The script on the new machine asks the adding station for the release when it has no `curl`, when the release page does not answer its probe, or when `install.sh` ends saying the release could not be had (exit 75: the newest release's name, the archive or its checksum file could not be downloaded, or the download did not match its published checksum). On Windows: the probe fails, or `install.ps1` throws "The release could not be downloaded". A "not found" for the archive on Windows is still "no release for this system". The install then goes on from the archive that was sent, by the same install script (`SPANDECK_ARCHIVE`, `SPANDECK_ARCHIVE_SHA256`, `SPANDECK_VERSION`: install from a file already on the machine, downloading nothing), and starts and joins as usual. It is the "add" job only; a station that is only made to ask ("Add X to this fleet", "Try again" after a failed join) installs nothing and is sent nothing.
- **The owner sees progress, not a failure.** Under the steps, not in the warning colour: "monitoring cannot reach GitHub: sending spandeck v0.4.11 from spark2 over SSH (18 MB)…", then "… 6 MB of 18 MB sent." every tenth of the way, then "Sent 18 MB to monitoring, which is checking and installing it." The same lines are in the log, with the machine's own "Received 18459210 bytes, and their SHA-256 matches."
- **Which version: the newest release, as before.** Add a machine has always installed the newest release (the install script asks the release page for "latest"), not the version of the station that adds; that rule is kept, so a release that is sent is the one the machine would have downloaded. The adding station asks its release host for the newest name. It never sends anything older than itself: if the host names an older release as its newest (a stale copy of the release page), the station asks it for its own version instead.
- **Which bytes, in this order.**
  1. *The published archive for the new machine's platform*, when the adding station reaches its release host. The host is the one in the code, or `[update] url` in that station's own config: nothing the new machine, a caller or another station says changes it. The station downloads the archive and the SHA-256 published beside it and refuses a mismatch: the same code, and so the same check, as `spandeck update` (`ReleaseHost` in `crates/core/src/supply.rs`, which `station_update` now uses). A mismatch is never sent and nothing else is tried: "spark2 downloaded the macOS (Apple silicon) build of v0.4.11 to send to monitoring, and it does not match its published checksum, so nothing was sent and nothing was installed."
  2. *The kept copy.* What was downloaded is kept under `supply/<version>/` in the state folder, for the two newest versions. Before it is sent again the published SHA-256 is fetched again and the kept file must match it; one that does not is deleted and downloaded afresh. When the release host does not answer, a kept copy that still matches the SHA-256 it was checked against, and is not older than the station, is sent, and the dialog says so ("sending the release spark2 downloaded and checked earlier").
  3. *The station's own copy, only for a machine of its own platform* (same system and processor), when the release host cannot be reached (or does not have the station's version) and nothing is kept: an archive of the program the station is running, laid out as a release is (`spandeck`, `sd`; on Windows also `spandeckd.exe` and the pane host), so the install script makes of it exactly what it makes of a release. `sd` is the same program under another name: the file beside the running one, or the running one. The dialog says "sending this station's own copy, 0.4.10". It is made for that one sending and deleted.
  3a. *(Added the same day, see the next section.)* Before its own copy, and for a machine of any kind: a station of the fleet that holds or can fetch the published release is asked for it, and it is sent on.
  4. *Otherwise nothing*, and the failure names the facts and what to do: "monitoring cannot reach GitHub, and neither can spark2 to fetch the macOS (Apple silicon) build; none of the stations asked (m4, studio) has it or can reach the release host. Add it when a station of your fleet can reach GitHub (any of them then supplies it), or from a station that is the same kind of machine, which sends its own copy. The same kind of machine as monitoring (macOS (Apple silicon)) in your fleet: m4, studio." (Each station says its system and processor in `station.info`.)
- **Nothing is taken from the new machine but the name of its platform**, which must be one of the five that have a release and of the system Tailscale gives for the machine. It asks once in a session; a second request is not answered. No bytes for this come from the new machine or from another station, and nothing is run on the adding station on the new machine's word: the station does what the owner started the job to do.
- **How it travels: in the one SSH session, on its standard input, after the script.** No second connection, so the password is still used for one connection; no extra port, no `scp` or `sftp`, no connection sharing (which the OpenSSH client on Windows does not have). What is relied on: `ssh -T host sh -s` hands standard input through byte for byte. **The script itself is not read by its shell from standard input** (changed 17 Nov 2026, after the first build hung on a real Mac). `sh -s` is given one line: it says `@spandeck script`, takes exactly the script's length in bytes with `head -c` (the station sends the script only after that line has spoken, so a shell that reads ahead, as dash does, has nothing to read ahead into), and runs it with `exec sh -c`. From then on standard input is the data's alone; the script keeps it as file descriptor 3 and closes it before spandeck is started. A script that is cut short is a function without its end and its call, and nothing of it runs. *Why:* the first build put the script on standard input and the data behind it, which dash and bash 5 allow. The `sh` of macOS is bash 3.2.57, which corrupts its own memory when the descriptor it reads its script from is duplicated (`3<&0`) and then read: on macOS 26 the shell crashed inside `free` and spun in its signal handler for ever (seen with `sample`: every sample in `_sigtramp` and malloc's abort path; a smaller script of the same shape ended with "Segmentation fault: 11"). The unit test was that hang, on the Mac and on GitHub's macOS runner; an add of a Mac that could not download would have hung until its time limit. Run on that Mac afterwards, in a pretend home under `/tmp`, with its `/bin/sh` (bash 3.2.57), `/bin/dash` and `/bin/zsh` as the shell that is given the line: sent whole (installed, started, asked to join), and cut short (refused, nothing left behind), with `shasum` (no `sha256sum` on the PATH) and BSD `head`, `wc`, `mktemp` and `tar`. The temporary folder is now made under `$TMPDIR` by name (`mktemp -d "${TMPDIR:-/tmp}/spandeck-release.XXXXXX"`): macOS's bare `mktemp -d` puts it elsewhere. The exchange: the machine says `@spandeck supply <platform> <why>`; the station answers one line, `release <version> <bytes> <sha256>`, or `none`; the machine makes a temporary file, says `@spandeck ready`, and takes exactly that many bytes (`head -c`).
- **Checked on arrival.** The machine compares the SHA-256 of what arrived with the one the station sent (the published one, or the station's own file's) before anything is unpacked, and the install script checks it again. Fewer bytes than announced (the connection dropped, the add was stopped) or other bytes: the file is deleted, nothing is installed, and the add ends with "What spark2 sent did not arrive whole on monitoring". The temporary folder is removed however the script ends.
- **Windows machines.** Windows PowerShell 5.1 reads its standard input as text, and the script used to be read to the end of input. For an install the line PowerShell is given is now `[Console]::In.ReadLine() | Invoke-Expression` (still no `$` and no quote); the first line sent reads the script up to `#spandeck-end-of-script` and runs it, and the input stays open. The archive goes as lines of base64 (48,000 bytes a line), read through the same reader until the announced number of bytes has been decoded, then checked by SHA-256 as on Unix. Joining and uninstalling still read to the end of input, as before. **This side has not been run**: nothing here runs PowerShell. It is checked as text, the base64 the station writes is checked to decode to the archive, and a test that runs the reader, the transfer, a short transfer and a refusal in Windows PowerShell exists and runs only on Windows (the CI job); a real Windows machine has not been added this way. `add_machine_cmd` for a Windows machine must now read one line and run it.
- **Time.** An add still has ten minutes, not counting the sending. The station has 7 minutes to get the release (a download, or its own files; after that "spark2 did not have the release to send to monitoring within seven minutes"); the sending may take 50 minutes and is stopped when the session takes nothing for 2 minutes ("Sending spandeck to monitoring over SSH stopped…"). 18 MB in 50 minutes is 6 KB a second.
- **By hand** (for trying it on a machine that can reach GitHub, and no page asks for it): `spandeck rpc fleet.add '{"machine": "<id from fleet.candidates>", "user": "<name>", "send": true}'` sends the release although the machine could download it; `"send": "own"` sends the station's own copy as if the release host did not answer. The owner's alone, like the rest of `fleet.add`.
- **Afterwards: such a station is updated through the fleet** (decided and built the same day: the next section). `spandeck update` there asks the stations of its fleet when it cannot download, and `spandeck doctor` says so. `spandeck doctor` asks the release host only when run from the command line, never when a page or a test asks for the checks.
- **For a host without Tailscale** (the design of 16 Nov 2026 for a locked-down company, which pushes the program over SSH): the three parts in `supply.rs` know nothing of Add a machine: which bytes (`release_for`), what the other end runs to take them (`unix_receive_lines`, `windows_receive_lines`), and how they go into a session's input (`send`). A link over SSH can use them as they are.
- **Mixed versions.** A station on 0.4.10 that does the adding sends the old script: no release is sent, and a machine that cannot reach GitHub fails as before. A new station adding installs whatever the newest release is, also an older one's, since the script and the install script are the adding station's own. Which stations are "the same kind of machine" is known for every version (`os` and `arch` have always been in `station.info`).
- **Safety.** See SECURITY.md. In short: a station that adds a machine already runs a script of its choosing there as the owner's user, so sending the program gives a compromised adding station nothing it did not have; what must not weaken is the published-checksum check where the release host is reachable, and it is the same code as an update's.

### A station that cannot download its update asks the fleet (17 Nov 2026, the owner's decision)

About updating a station that cannot reach the release host: "I think updating, if the target machine can't get it via git then it should ask the closest server to serve it up for them, any machine should be able to get the targets [build] from git right?" That is right, and no SSH and no password are involved: the station is already in the fleet. It replaces "updating such a station is not built" in the section before this one.

- **The station being updated asks, for itself.** `station.update` (the deck's "Update to the latest release", `spandeck update`, `update --station X`, and the bar that updates the stations that are behind: all are this one call, made on the station that is to update) first asks its release host for the newest release, with three seconds to answer. When it answers, everything is as before. When it does not (or the download then breaks off, or the checksum cannot be fetched), the station asks the stations of its fleet for the release for its own platform, checks what arrives, and upgrades in place exactly as from a download: the same unpacking, the same health check, and "a version that does not start properly is undone". A download that does not match its published checksum, and a release the host says it does not have, are still errors and the fleet is not asked.
- **The call answers at once; a notice says how it goes.** The answer is "LLM-Server cannot reach GitHub: asking the stations of your fleet for the release…" (the deck shows it; another station that passed the request on waits only five seconds for an answer, which is why the release host is first asked with three). One notice, not sent to the phone, whose words are replaced as it goes: "LLM-Server cannot reach GitHub: getting v0.4.11 from spark2 (18 MB)…", "… getting v0.4.11 from spark2: 6 MB of 18 MB…", and at the end "… got v0.4.11 from spark2 (18 MB) and is updating to it. Terminals stay open, and a version that does not start properly is undone." or why not. The ship's log has what was taken, from whom and its SHA-256 (`station.update`), and each station that was passed over and why (`release.supply`).
- **Which station: the closest that can.** Every station of the fleet that answers now and says it supplies releases (`release_supply` in what it says of itself) is asked `release.offer`: can you supply this release (or your newest) for this platform? Each answers without downloading anything: `kept` (it holds the archive, and it still matches the SHA-256 it was checked against), `fetch` (its release host answers and publishes that file; it says the published SHA-256), `own` (it runs that version on that kind of machine), or `none`. The newest release offered is taken, from a station that holds it before one that would have to fetch it, and among equals from the one with the shortest round trip (the Performance page's figure for the link, `rtt_now`: no second measure was added), a station never timed last. When a station fails, the next that offered is taken from. No station is special: every station answers these calls the same way.
- **How it travels.** Over the signed station-to-station calls and nothing else: `release.prepare` (have it ready: the station downloads it if need be and checks it against its published SHA-256; up to five and a half minutes), then `release.chunk` for one piece after another from an offset. A piece begins at 128 KB, is halved when it took more than two seconds (down to 16 KB) and doubled when it took less than half a second (up to 1 MB), may take 30 seconds, and is asked for again from the same place twice before the next station is tried; the whole may take 50 minutes. The pieces go on connections of their own, one at a time, and after each the link is left a quarter of the time the piece took: a message, a stop, a round of the fleet or a terminal between the two stations never waits behind a piece (measured twice: some 310 rounds of two ordinary calls during a transfer of the real program between two stations on one machine, median 1.4 and 1.5 ms, slowest 68 ms and 430 ms; the test allows a second). These calls are not counted in the link's figures on the Performance page, and a piece that fails does not mark the station as silent.
- **What the station that asks checks, and what it has to believe.** It cannot ask the release host (that is the situation), so it believes a station of its fleet that says "this is the SHA-256 I checked against the published one". That is no new power: a station of the fleet can already act as the owner on every other (SECURITY.md). What it checks all the same, since it costs nothing: the bytes have the SHA-256 that was offered, and the size; what was made ready is the version and platform asked for, and the archive holds `spandeck-<version>-<platform>/` or it is not installed; **two stations that hold or can fetch the same release must give the same SHA-256, or nothing is taken from either** ("spark2 and m4 give different SHA-256s for v0.4.11, so one of them holds something that is not that release…"), which finds one spoiled copy; and nothing older than the station is taken unless the owner named that version (`spandeck update v0.4.9`), as for a download.
- **What a station hands on.** Only an archive it checked against its published SHA-256: downloaded now, or kept from before and matching the SHA-256 published now, or, when its release host does not answer, matching the one it was checked against. A station that was itself supplied by the fleet keeps no copy and so hands none on. The one exception: **when no station holds or can fetch a release**, a station of the same kind sends its own running program, laid out as a release (as for Add a machine), and the notice says "got spark2's own copy, v0.4.11 from spark2". Since an own copy of the same version is no update, this happens when that station runs something newer, or when the owner names the version.
- **Only a station asks.** `release.offer`, `release.prepare` and `release.chunk` are on the stations' list and no other: no agent and no read-only caller has them, the owner's own connection is refused them too, and a browser cannot reach the stations' endpoint at all.
- **`spandeck doctor`** on a station that cannot reach the release host: "this station cannot reach the release host (GitHub); updates come through the fleet (last supplied by spark2 on 17 Nov 2026: v0.4.11): `spandeck update` asks its stations", or, before any was needed, which stations can supply one; or, when none can, that and what to do until one can.
- **Add a machine uses it too.** A station that is adding a machine of another kind and cannot reach the release host asks the fleet for that kind's release before giving up, and sends it on over SSH: "monitoring cannot reach GitHub, and neither can spark2: sending the release m4 fetched and checked, v0.4.11, from spark2 over SSH (18 MB)…". Only a release a station checked against its published SHA-256 is passed on this way, never another station's own copy. So "add it from a station that is the same kind of machine" is needed only when no station of the fleet can reach the release host, and the failure now reads: "monitoring cannot reach GitHub, and neither can spark2 to fetch the macOS (Apple silicon) build; none of the stations asked (m4, studio) has it or can reach the release host. Add it when a station of your fleet can reach GitHub (any of them then supplies it), or from a station that is the same kind of machine, which sends its own copy. …".
- **Mixed versions.** A station from before this (0.4.10, 0.4.11) cannot ask: it updates in the ordinary way if it can reach the release host, and if it cannot, one last time by the road of the section before (uninstall it from another station, which keeps its history and settings, and add it again; or copy an archive to it and run `spandeck upgrade`). It is never asked to supply, since it does not say it can; when no station that answers can, the update says so at once: "LLM-Server cannot reach GitHub to download the release, and no station of your fleet can supply releases yet (m4, studio run a spandeck from before stations could: update one of them first). …".
- **Not run:** on Windows and on macOS (the calls are the same code everywhere; packing a station's own copy on Windows uses the system's `tar`, and is the part most likely to differ), and between real machines over Tailscale, where the adapting piece size and the pauses matter and were not seen at work.

### Is there a newer spandeck? (19 Nov 2026, the owner's request)

"how do I know if there is a spandeck update?" The deck said so only when one station of the fleet already ran a newer version than another. Nothing said a release had been published while every station was on the same older one.

- **Each station asks its release host for the newest release's name, about once a day.** The host is the one `station.update` takes releases from: the one in the code, or `[update] url` in that station's config. The station looks five minutes after it starts and every hour after that, and asks when its last good answer is a day old. It also asks when the owner does (`update.check`: "Check for a newer spandeck now" in a station's menu, which asks every station that answers, and `spandeck update --check`). Nothing is downloaded or installed by asking; an update is still the owner's act.
- **What is asked.** One GET of `<releases>/latest`, through the HTTP client every outside call uses (`http.rs`), with three seconds to connect and eight for the answer. The answer is a redirect to the newest release's page, and the release's name is the last part of it. The redirect is read and not followed, so nothing else is asked of anyone. The request carries nothing of the owner, the station or a project, and not the version the station runs: the host learns that a machine at this address asked for a public version number.
- **How long an answer is kept.** A good answer a day. A check that failed is tried again after an hour. A station says "latest" only of an answer of the last three days, so one that was asleep for a week does not say what it knew before.
- **"Could not check" is never "latest".** A check that fails is said with its reason ("could not check: no route to github.com"), and what an earlier good answer said is not said again as if it were new. A release an earlier answer named is still named, since it was published: after "0.4.19 available", a host that stops answering leaves "0.4.19 available".
- **The switch.** `[update] check = false` in a station's config, or the switch `update_check` set to false (`[flags]`, `station.flags`), turns it off there: nothing is asked, and the deck says "checks are off" where it would say the result.
- **No master station.** Each station says what it learned in `station.info` (`update`). The fleet's view is the newest release any station of it learned, worked out by whichever station serves the deck (`update` in `fleet.view`): one station that reaches a release host is enough. A station that cannot reach its own (one that takes its releases from the fleet) has no answer of its own and is still told "0.4.19 available".
- **What comes back is data.** A release's name is a version (two to four numbers with dots, and at most a short pre-release part) or it is thrown away. What another station says is read the same way, and its reason is cut to a line. Versions are compared as versions (`supply::older`). The link to the release's page is made by the station that serves the deck, from its own release address and the checked version (`<releases>/tag/v0.4.19`). An address in the answer, or one another station gives, is never used. The deck shows the version as text.
- **The line in the deck.** When a newer release is out and every station runs the same version: one quiet line, "spandeck 0.4.19 is available.", the version a link to the release's page (a new tab, `rel="noopener"`), "Update all stations" and "Not now". Update asks first and then makes the call the version bar makes (`station.update`) on every station that answers and is behind. "Not now" is remembered for that version in that browser; the next release is said again.
- **One bar, not two.** When the stations already differ, the bar of "The deck says when the stations do not all run the same version" is the one shown. It adds "spandeck 0.4.19 is available: an update takes every station to it", and its Update covers every station that is behind the release, not only those behind the newest in the fleet. Its "Not now" is as before.
- **On a phone** the line is one line that never wraps ("Update", "Not now"), and it is not drawn while a pane is open, so a conversation is never moved by it. It is on the lists.
- **Where it can always be seen.** Beside each station's version, wherever stations are listed with one: the station's tile, the Version figure of the Performance page, and `spandeck fleet`, which ends with a line when a release is out. The words are "latest", "0.4.19 available", "could not check: …", "checks are off" or "not checked yet". A station with the check off says "checks are off" unless another station learned of a release it is behind.
- **Who may ask.** `update.check` is the owner's alone (rule 7): it makes a station call a host outside, so an agent, a read-only caller and another station cannot.
- **Mixed versions.** A station from before this says nothing of releases and is not asked. It is still told "0.4.19 available" by a station that knows.
- **Not built:** a notice or a message to the phone when a release comes out. The line and the words beside the versions are the whole of it.
- **Not run:** against the real release host. The tests use a listener on the same machine that redirects as GitHub's "latest" page does. With `[update] url` set to a mirror, the link is `<url>/tag/v…`, which a mirror may not serve.

## Adding a station that is running, and uninstalling one (15 Nov 2026, the owner's request)

"I dont know how to add these two machines, I shouldnt have to go to that machine, I should just say add to the fleet but its asking me to join their fleet." "I also cant delete the machine then re-add it."

- **"Add X to this fleet…"** is on the tile and in the menu of a station that is running but is not in this fleet. It replaces the instruction to go to that machine and type `spandeck fleet --join`, and it replaces "Join X's fleet…" and "Merge this fleet into X's…", which are no longer on any Control Deck menu: the owner adds to the fleet they are looking at. `spandeck fleet --join` stays in the command line, unchanged.
- It is Add a machine without the install (`fleet.add` with a station's name). The station the owner is using connects to the other over SSH, which is what shows the request is the owner's; finds the spandeck command there (where the installer puts it, then the PATH, then `~/.cargo/bin`); checks it answers; and has it ask to join this station, as `spandeck fleet --join` does. The code it is given appears here and is approved here, with the usual control. The switch, the user name, the password, the host key and the progress are those of Add a machine.
- **Its fleet-mates come with it**, because a join is a merge. The dialog says so beforehand, and names them once the request has arrived. A station tells whether it brings its mates in what it says of itself to any device under the owner's login (`station.hello`: `merge`, with its operating system). One that does not say so runs a version from before the merge: the dialog says it will join alone and that any station it shares a fleet with stays behind, to be added the same way.
- **The address** is the one this station found that station at, and it must be a Tailscale address or one the owner wrote under `peers` in this station's config. A caller names a station, never an address.
- **"Uninstall spandeck on X…"** is in the menu of every other station that answers (`fleet.uninstall`). In order:
  1. If X is in this fleet, it is removed from it exactly as "Remove this station from the fleet" does: its panes closed, a new key for the stations that stay, X forgotten. The browser that asked gets its new token in the reply.
  2. Over SSH, spandeck on X is told what a removal with "also stop spandeck there" tells it (`station.leave` with `stop`, through its own command line): it closes its panes, takes its login service out and ends. That code touches the login service only when the account it runs under has one.
  3. The script deletes the programs: `spandeck` and `sd` where the installer put them, or where the command was found if it was built from source (on Windows `spandeck.exe`, `sd.exe` and `spandeckd.exe`), and the copies kept for upgrades under the state folder.
- **Kept by default: the state and config folders**, which hold the station's history, archive and settings, so that adding the machine back picks them up. The tick box "Erase everything" deletes them too; a folder is deleted only if it is spandeck's (it holds the database, the key or the config, or is named `spandeck`), and never the home folder. Helpers' worktrees live under the state folder and go with it. **The owner's project folders are never touched**, with or without the box.
- If spandeck was not running on X, the script takes the login service out itself, and only if the service's own file is in that account. It never stops a process by name. No test reaches a real login service: the test's machine has stand-ins for `systemctl` and `launchctl` first on its PATH and a home folder of its own, and the test checks the stand-ins were what was asked.
- If the SSH step fails after step 1 (a password is needed, say), nothing has been deleted on X: it is running, outside the fleet, and the same dialog asks for the password and goes on from step 2. A station that does not answer cannot be uninstalled: "Remove" takes it out from the fleet's side. The station the owner is using cannot uninstall itself.
- The dialog names the station and its pane counts, opens on Cancel and acts only on the red button. Afterwards the machine is listed under Add a machine again.
- **Authority.** `fleet.add` and `fleet.uninstall` are the owner's alone, on no agent's list and no station's, never forwarded in either direction, and refused unless adding machines is turned on at the station being used (`fleet.add_enable`, or `add_machines = true`). The ship's log records who, which station, which user and how it went (`fleet.add`, `fleet.uninstall`).
- **Browsers signed out in a fleet stay signed out when stations arrive.** The key is handed over with the list of browsers signed out in that fleet, at a join and with `fleet.rekey`, and the station that takes the key refuses them too. Before, a station that joined accepted every token made with the key, including one the owner had signed out. A station on an older version ignores the list.
- After a request to join, the asking station looks for its answer every 0.4 seconds for the first 16 seconds, then every 1.5 seconds as before.

## Usage and cost (14 Nov 2026, the owner's request)

- Token counts are only what an agent wrote in its own session data: Claude Code's and Codex's session files, OpenCode's session database. Antigravity keeps nothing spandeck can read, and is shown as not reported. Nothing is typed in by the owner.
- Counted per conversation and per model, sub-agents included under the model that ran them; a model call written more than once is counted once. A new conversation starts from zero; the station keeps its totals.
- Cost is an estimate at list prices and is called that wherever it appears, with the list's source and date: a subscription is not billed per token.
- Prices and context windows come from a public list (models.dev) that spandeckd fetches by itself once a day and keeps. The request carries nothing about the owner or a project. No price or window size is written into the code; a model the list does not name has no price. `[prices]` in the config is an optional override, never needed.
- The context gauge uses the window the session data states (Codex), else the list's.

## Two agents on one project (18 Nov 2026, from a real run of a builder and a critic)

Decided after the owner's test of Claude Code as builder and Codex as critic, and their
instruction to build what the report recommended.

- **The pair is the second recipe, and sets a project up.** `spandeck project new --recipe
  pair` (`project.new` with `recipe`, the owner's; no agent, station or read-only caller has
  it) makes the folder and its git repository, writes `PROCESS.md` (from
  `crates/core/src/recipes/pair-process.md`) and `BRIEF.md`, never over a file that is
  there, defines the panes `builder` and `critic` in the project's own folder (roles placed
  `same-tree`: both work in one tree, and the critic keeps to `reviews/` by the process, not
  by enforcement), starts them, and sends each its role as one message from the owner. With
  no brief nobody is told to start; the roles are sent when the project is next opened with
  a brief written. (That message is now the role's prompt from the role library, and a
  third pane, `user`, can be added: see Roles for agents.) It answers none of an agent
  program's prompts. A first commit is made
  only in a repository with none, of the files written, as `spandeck <spandeck@localhost>`
  when no author is configured; no git configuration is written. `recipe.list` is the
  review loops.
- **Activity is not a note.** What agents post on purpose (finding, decision, note, status)
  and what spandeck captures from hooks (kind `auto`: a file edited, a command that failed,
  a test run) are kept apart end to end. `context_search` returns posted notes unless
  activity is asked for, and every result says which it is. The start digest gives the
  notes first and the activity as one line for each agent. A pane's edit of a file it edited
  within ten minutes is one row. Nobody posts activity. A linked project is never shown
  another project's activity. No migration: old rows are read as they are.
- **A project's record is written by the station.** `project.record`, the owner's alone,
  and `spandeck project record`: one Markdown document, and the same as data, built only
  from the ship's log, the messages, the notes, the approvals and the usage totals. Times
  are UTC. Text from agents is only ever in a fenced block or a code span. "What looks
  wrong" lists counts against stated limits and no opinion. What the station does not record
  is said in the document, never filled in.
  - **It holds this project's and no other's** (19 Nov 2026: the record of `hero3` held
    eight messages of `pairtest`, whose panes had the same names, and began at that
    project's time). A message is the project's when one of its panes sent it or it was
    sent to one: by the pane's id or by `project/name`, never by a pane's name. A message
    stored with a bare name, as they were until the station wrote the project into the
    address, counts only when a pane of the project had the name at that time and no other
    pane did; where two had it, the message is left out and the document says how many.
  - **One row for each name.** A pane that ended and whose name another pane of the
    project has since (a critic that exited and was replaced) is not a row of its own: its
    time in each state, its tool calls, its approvals and its tokens are counted in the
    row of the pane that has the name now, which names the earlier panes by id
    (`earlier_panes`). With no pane of the name running, the row is the newest. It is
    folded, not listed apart, so that a name's time in its states still sums to the wall
    time; the pane that took the name is not "marked exited and running again".
  - **Tokens, cost and turns are the period's, to the hour.** The station counts each
    conversation's tokens by the hour (the archive's `usage_hours`), so a pane's are those
    of the whole hours that hold the period, first event to last, and the document names
    the hours. (They were the pane's whole life, and the document said so.) No migration:
    a cut finer than the hour would need the time of each model call kept.
- **A bare pane name is a pane of the caller's own project first** (19 Nov 2026: `spandeck
  send critic` from a pane of `hero3` failed because `pairtest` on the same station had a
  `critic` too). In every call of an agent's that names a pane (`msg.send`, `wait`,
  `pane.get`, `pane.text`, `diff`, `orders.view`) a name with no project is looked for among
  the running panes of the calling pane's project, and only when that project has none on
  the whole station. A name several panes still share is refused with the full address of
  each to choose from (`project/name@station`; two panes one address would name, by id).
  The owner outside any pane is answered as before, with that list. Who may reach whom is
  unchanged: another project's pane still needs the link. A message sent to a bare name is
  stored under the pane's full name (`project/name@station`) when the pane is in a project:
  mail is kept by name, and a `critic` of another project is never shown it. Not changed:
  a bare name sent to another station is resolved there as before, by that station.
- **Watch is the conversation.** A project's Watch shows its agents as a strip (state, how
  long, one line of what each is doing; no terminal thumbnail), who is waiting for whom
  when that can be told from the messages and the states and never otherwise, and the
  messages between them as one thread, newest last. (The owner, of the map of cards and
  lines it replaces: "Hard to tell what's really happening between agents".)
- **The page is not taken by a flood.** Whole-page redraws from the station's news are at
  most two a second with a burst of four, and wait while the owner is scrolling. A notice
  that repeats for the same pane and reason within eight seconds, whatever its timer says,
  is one notice. A pane whose state changes six times in ten seconds says so in one line and
  is reported once (`client.report`, `state-flood`). What the owner did, and what they are
  reading or typing, is never held back. (A pane's state flipped each second for minutes
  and the owner's phone could not be scrolled.)
- **A program's own review is its own.** A pane whose program was started to review for
  itself what it would otherwise ask the owner (Codex's `--approve-for-me`, set by the
  permission mode `auto`) is not held for the owner by the station's hook. The permission
  mode set for an agent reaches Codex as it reaches Claude Code and Antigravity, and is
  chosen in Agent setup.

## Roles for agents (18 Nov 2026, the owner's request)

The owner: "I wonder if we should have roles defined when we start a agent. So a critic has a defined role it should perform across UX design, architecture, system design, performance, code review etc." And then: "Ok be detailed on the prompt for the role, I should be able to view and change the role prompt".

What was there before: a pane could carry a role's name (`pane.spawn` with `role`, a project's pane slot, `delegate`), which chose a helper's placement and which standing orders applied, and said nothing to the agent. The pair recipe sent each of its two agents one sentence. The second run of a pair (`herotest-report/REPORT.md`, 18 Nov 2026) found that the critic "reviewed evidence, not the product".

**Built**: the station side (the calls are `docs/roles-api.md`), the command line (`spandeck roles`, `pane new --role`), the Control Deck's Roles dialog, and the pair recipe's use of it.

- **A role library, the same on every station** (`crates/core/src/roledoc.rs`). A role has a name (letters, digits, `-`, `_`; 32 at most), a title, a one-line summary, a prompt (markdown, 16 KB at most), a kind (`standing`: on the team for the whole job; `visit`: one review, then done), an optional budget line, and optional defaults: the program, the model, the effort and the permission mode of a pane started with it.
- **Seven roles are built in**, their prompts the files in `crates/core/src/roles/`: builder, critic, user (standing), and architect, performance, accessibility, security (a visit). The critic's begins with using the product as its user would and holding it against the brief, before any code.
- **The document holds only what the owner changed or added** (`roles.json`, by the rules of the other synced documents: The same view from every station). A built-in role nobody changed is not in it, so **an unchanged built-in role follows the release**: a later spandeck that improves the text improves it on every station at that station's update. A built-in role the owner changed is in it whole and marked changed; it no longer follows the release, the built-in text stays readable beside it, and "reset to the built-in text" takes the owner's out. A built-in role saved exactly as the release has it is not kept. A built-in role cannot be removed, only reset; a role of the owner's own can be removed.
- **It is a document of its own**, for the reason the tools document is (Tools for agents): a field added to the agent setup would be dropped by every station on 0.4.15 and by every station that took its copy from one.
- **A prompt comes from the station's library and from nowhere else.** A role's prompt is sent to an agent as the owner's instruction. A project's file (`.spandeck/project.toml`) travels with a repository, so it may **name** a role and can never supply a role's prompt, title or budget: the file has no place for them, and what it writes under a role's name is not read. What is put into a prompt when it is sent is checked to be a name and no more: a pane's name or a role's name from a project's file that is anything but letters, digits, `-`, `_` (and `.` in a pane's name) is left out of the sentence.
- **Placeholders are filled when the prompt is sent, and shown as written when it is viewed**: `{{project}}`; `{{others}}`, a short paragraph naming the project's other agent panes and the role of each, or "You are working alone in this project."; `{{budget}}`, the role's budget or the one given at the start. An unknown `{{…}}` is left as written, and what is put in is not read for placeholders again.
- **A pane started with a role is sent that role's prompt once**, when the role's name is in the library: `pane.spawn` with `role`, a project's pane slot (`project.open`), a helper started by `delegate`, a recipe. It is a message from the owner that begins "Your role in this project, from the owner:", by the road every message takes and under its rules (never typed into a program's own question; held while the pane has only just started). A name the library does not have sends nothing, as before. Restarted into its conversation, restored after the station's restart, or reopened by its project into the conversation it had, a pane is not sent it again; started on a new conversation, it is. The owner sends it again with `roles.send` ("Send its role again" in the pane's menu). The ship's log records `role.sent` with the pane, the role and whether the text was the built-in or the owner's.
- **A role's defaults.** Its model, effort and permission mode are laid over Agent setup's for a pane started with the role; its program is used when a pane is started with the role and no program is named, and by `delegate` for a role the project's file says nothing of.
- **An agent can read its own role again** (`role`, and the MCP tool of that name): the filled prompt of the calling pane's role, or that it has none. An agent has no call that lists, changes or sends roles, and is answered for its own pane whatever it names.
- **Every `roles.` call is the owner's alone** (rule 7): `roles.list`, `roles.get`, `roles.set`, `roles.reset`, `roles.remove`, `roles.send` are on no list of an agent's, a read-only caller's or a station's. A station has one, `roles.push`, to offer its copy of the document. Another station is reached as the owner's forwarded request.
- **A save made to an older copy says so.** `roles.set` carries the stamp of the role as the caller read it; when the role was changed since, nothing is saved and the answer says who changed it. The dialog keeps the owner's text in the box, says "Not saved", and offers to save it over the other.
- **The pair recipe uses the library.** Its builder and critic are sent their library role (filled, naming the others) followed by the recipe's line ("Read BRIEF.md and PROCESS.md in this folder, then start stage 0…"), in one message each. `--user [<agent>]` adds a third pane, `user`, with the role `user` (its program: the builder's, unless named). `--budget <minutes>` (5 to 240; 20 unless said) is the time a stage gets, written into `PROCESS.md` and into each agent's role. `PROCESS.md` was rewritten to the lessons of the second run: the critic's review begins with using the product and holding it against the brief; one round a stage unless there is a blocker; the plan carries one acceptance list, and cuts are decided there; with a `user` pane, it looks at each slice after it is committed and the builder answers its three things like findings. The separate UX stage is gone: the critic uses the product at every slice.

### The Roles dialog (18 Nov 2026)

The screen for the above (`crates/web/assets/roles.js`), built on `docs/roles-api.md`. It is reached from the top bar ("Roles", beside Tools), the command palette and, on a phone, the top bar's menu.

- **The list**: each role's title, name, kind ("on the team", "a short visit"), summary, and "changed by you" or "yours" where it is.
- **A role opened** shows its whole prompt as preformatted text that wraps (text, never HTML), its budget and defaults, Edit, "Copy to a new role", and for a changed built-in "Reset to the built-in text" and "Show the built-in text".
- **Edit**: a large box with the prompt, the title, the summary, the kind, the budget and the defaults; which words are filled in and what each becomes; a count against the 16 KB limit; a word in `{{…}}` that will not be filled in is said. Save and Cancel; on a phone they stay at the foot.
- **What is typed is kept apart from what is drawn**: the dialog being drawn again (the list read again, another station's change arriving) never replaces the box being typed in, and closing the dialog over unsaved text asks first, inside the dialog.
- **New role**: empty, or "Copy from" another role.
- **Where a role is chosen**: the New pane dialog and the phone's New session sheet have "Role" (none unless chosen) for an agent, with the role's summary under it; the pane's own line says its role beside its program; the pane's menu has "Send its role again" when it has one.
- **Every action says what it did**, and a button that asks the station is greyed until it answers.
- **On a phone** the dialog is a sheet, every target is 44 px, and nothing is wider than the page at 320 px.

**What an older station does.** A station from before this (0.4.15) has no `roles` in `caps`: it is never offered the document and cannot erase it; it sends no pane a role's prompt, and a pair it sets up gets the one sentence it always sent; it knows none of the `roles.` calls. A deck does not offer "Role" for a pane on such a station, nor "Send its role again". A later format of the document is not taken by a station that does not read it (`format`), as with preferences and tools.

**Not known.**

- **Whether the roles make a pair's product better.** No run was made with them. The prompts and the process answer what the second run's report found; that they do so in practice is not established.
- **Real agent programs.** Nothing here was run with a real Claude Code or Codex: that a 4 KB role arriving as a message is read in full after the program's first screen, and that the `role` tool is used after a compaction, were not seen.
- **A role's defaults and each program's words.** A model, an effort or a permission mode is passed as Agent setup's is; a word the program does not know may stop it from starting, exactly as there. For Codex a permission mode that is not auto, on-request or never is not passed.
- **A helper and its role.** A helper started by `delegate` begins on its brief and is told of its role as mail, which it reads at its first hook. Whether the role should instead be part of the brief was not decided.
- **The built-in prompts on a phone.** Their lines are broken at about 90 characters in the files, so on a narrow screen a line wraps and then breaks again. They are shown and sent exactly as stored; whether to store them one paragraph a line was left to the owner.

**Tests** (`crates/core/src/roledoc.rs`, `rpc.rs`, `crates/cli/tests/roles.rs`, `pair.rs`). The document's rules (a changed built-in is kept and marked, reset restores it, a role of the owner's own can be removed and a built-in cannot, limits refused); placeholders filled, an unknown one left; a project's file cannot supply a prompt, nor put more than a name into one; no agent, read-only caller or station has a `roles.` call, and an agent reads only its own role. Merge tier: a pane started with a role is sent it once and not into its first question, and again when the owner asks; the pair recipe with `--user` makes three panes, each sent its own role naming the others; two stations converge on an edited role and a save made to an older copy is refused; in a real browser at 390 px, 320 px and a desktop's width: the list, a role's whole prompt, edit and save, "changed by you", reset, a new role by copying, a role chosen where a pane is started and the pane sent it, and unsaved text surviving a redraw, the Close and a save that lost.

## The files of a station (19 Nov 2026, the owner's request)

The owner: a panel in the Control Deck that shows the files of a pane's station; change directories, a tree, copy and paste files of any type in, create, rename, delete; drag from the desktop to a station and from a station to the desktop; select several; copy and drag between stations, "which always creates copies"; and a separate "Move agent here" button that restarts the agent in the folder being looked at. Browsing never moves the agent. Deleting goes to a trash with Undo. One fleet, no master station, a slow station never slows the others, and the owner mostly uses an iPhone.

**Built**: the station side (`crates/core/src/files.rs`, `rpc.rs`, `daemon.rs`; `crates/web/src/files.rs`, `lanes.rs`), and the page (`crates/web/assets/files.js`; see The panel, below). The calls the page is built from are written down below, exactly; the page added none. No Guarantee is changed, no migration is added, and `PROTOCOL` is unchanged; a station that has these methods says `files` in `caps`.

### The rules

- **Whose.** Every `files.` method is the owner's alone. None is on the agents' list, the stations' list or the read-only list: a file can hold a secret, and so can its name. Another station reaches them only as the owner's forwarded request (`rpc.forward`, which there was already); no method was added for a station. An agent's token, a station calling for itself and a read-only caller are refused (`denied`).
- **What can be reached** is whatever the station's own user can reach: the daemon runs as that user, and the owner at a terminal there could do the same. Nothing is fenced to a pane's folder or to the home folder. Three things are refused all the same, because the explorer would be sawing off its own branch: trashing, deleting, renaming or moving away the root of a disk, the home folder itself, or a folder spandeck's own state is in (`refused`).
- **Nothing in use is deleted** (the owner, 19 Nov 2026: "I think we should not delete anything that is in use, we would ask the user to close those panes before deleting. If no panes are open then we could delete panes and projects"). Decided at the station, so it holds from any deck and any client: `files.trash`, `files.delete`, `files.rename` and a `move` by `files.transfer` refuse an item while any pane the station still lists (running, or ended and not yet closed) has its folder at that path or below it. **There is no way to force it**: no parameter, no second call. The item is not touched; the other items of the same call that are free go ahead. The refusal is an answer, not an error (code `in_use`), and carries the panes (`panes: [{id, name, project, ended}]`) and a sentence that names them ("builder and critic (project tally) are working in … or a folder inside it. Close those panes first"). A copy is never refused: it removes nothing. Paths are compared a part at a time and as the disk resolves them, so `/a/b` in use holds `/a` and `/a/b` and not `/a/bc`; a link to a folder is only a link, held only by a pane whose folder is written through it. With no pane there a folder goes like any other, a project's folder included (below, A project whose folder is gone). **The limit**: the station knows the folder a pane was started in, or was moved to by "Move agent here"; it does not know where a shell has since `cd`'d, nor what files a program has open. A shell started in `/work` that has gone into `/work/site` does not hold `/work/site` if that is deleted alone.
- **Paths are absolute**, written the station's own way (`/home/x` or `C:\Users\x`; `files.home` says the separator and the roots). Only `std::fs` is used; there is no platform code outside `crates/terminal/src/sys/` beyond reading and setting permission bits, telling whether two names are one file, and the Windows "hidden" bit, each behind `cfg` as `core` already does elsewhere.
- **A name is checked** before anything is touched: not empty, at most 255 bytes, not `.` or `..`, no `/` and no `\` (on any system: a name that is good on one station is good on every station), no NUL, and not beginning `.spandeck-part.`. What the system then refuses (a `:` on Windows) is the system's error.
- **Names and contents are data.** Nothing is interpreted, run, unpacked or looked into, except the first twelve bytes for a preview (below). The page inserts every name as text (AGENTS.md rule 6): an agent can name a file anything.
- **A name that is not UTF-8** cannot be said in a request. It is listed, with the bytes that are not UTF-8 shown as `�` and `odd_name: true`, and cannot be opened, renamed or deleted by itself through here. It still goes with its folder: a copy or a move on the same station keeps its exact name; a copy to another station and a zip leave it out and say so (`name`).
- **Whatever writes a file writes a part file first**, `.spandeck-part.<id>` in the folder the file is going to, and renames it into place: the name appears whole or not at all, and the rename never crosses a disk. Part files are not listed, not copied and not zipped. **None is left for good**: each is on a list the station keeps (`files/parts.log` under its state folder, one line per change, read again at the start), and one that nothing has written to for 24 hours is removed, at the start and every ten minutes.
- **Refusals the page must tell apart are answers, not errors.** A call between stations carries an error's words and not its code, and a kept answer given to a second try keeps only the codes it knows. So where the page has to decide what to do next (the name is taken, a piece is missing), the answer is `{"ok": false, "code": …, "message": …}`, the same through any number of stations. Everything else is an error as everywhere (`invalid`, `not_found`, `denied`, `internal`), with a sentence.
- **Delete is the trash.** `files.trash` moves each item into `files/trash/<id>/` under the station's state folder, with where it came from; `files.restore` puts them back. Kept 7 days, then removed. An item that cannot be moved there because it is on another disk is **not deleted**: it is listed with `other_disk`, the page asks "delete for good?", and `files.delete` does that. (So Undo is always a rename, never a copy of gigabytes the owner did not ask for.)
- **Between stations files are copied, never moved.** The station that has the files pushes them: it forwards the owner's `files.mkdir` and `files.put` to the other one. It is carrying out the owner's request; no station is granted anything, since any station of the fleet could already forward the owner's request. To copy from another station to this one the page asks that station (through this one) to copy `to` this one. A station that has no `files.` methods (an older spandeck) is told of: the job fails with "… does not have the file explorer yet: it needs updating to this version of spandeck before files can be copied to it".
- **A download can never run as a page of the deck.** It goes out as `application/octet-stream`, as an attachment, with `nosniff`, `Content-Security-Policy: default-src 'none'; sandbox` and `Cross-Origin-Resource-Policy: same-origin`. The one exception is a preview: with `inline=1`, a file whose first bytes say PNG, JPEG, GIF or WebP goes out inline as that type, and so does a film or a sound by its first bytes (21 Nov 2026, below: MP4 and M4V, QuickTime, M4A, WebM, MP3, WAV, Ogg), still under the same policy. What a file is is decided by its first bytes alone, never by its name, and also when only a later part of it is asked for. SVG and HTML are attachments always, whatever their names say and whatever is asked.
- **Nothing slow is waited for.** Every `files.` call does its work off the threads that answer everything else. A copy, a move and a zip are jobs, answered at once with an id; at most 8 run on a station at a time, and a ninth is refused with a sentence. A piece of a file between stations (`files.put`, `files.read`) travels on the connections kept for large things (`call_bulk`, as a release does), so nothing else said between the two stations waits behind it and a slow piece does not make a station out to be silent.
- **The ship's log** has each change: what, where, by whom, how many and how many bytes (`files.uploaded`, `files.folder_made`, `files.created`, `files.renamed`, `files.trashed`, `files.restored`, `files.deleted`, `files.copied`, `files.moved`), and never what a file holds. It does hold paths, so `log.query` shows file names to whoever may read the log. A change made through another station says so (`you (via alpha)`). Looking, reading and downloading are not logged.

### The calls

All are the owner's. Through another station: `peer.call {station, method, params}`, as for everything. `path` and `dir` are absolute paths on the station that answers.

**An entry**, wherever one is given: `{name, kind, size, mtime_ms, hidden, mode?, target?, odd_name?}`. `kind` is `dir`, `file`, `link` or `other` (a device, a socket). A `link` says what it leads to in `target` (`dir`, `file`, `other`, `missing`) and has the size and time of that. `size` is 0 for anything but a file. `hidden`: the name begins with a dot, or on Windows the file is marked hidden. `mode`: the permission bits as a number (`0o644` is 420), where the system has them.

| Call | Parameters | Answer |
|---|---|---|
| `files.home` | `pane?` | `{station, home, cwd, roots, sep, os, piece_max}`. `cwd`: the folder of that pane, null with no pane. `roots`: `["/"]`, or the drives (`["C:\\", "D:\\"]`). `os`: `linux`, `macos`, `windows`. `piece_max`: 4194304 |
| `files.list` | `path` | `{path, parent, entries, total, truncated}`. Folders (and links to folders) first, then by name without regard to case. At most 5,000 entries; `truncated` says there were more, `total` how many were seen (the station stops looking at 100,000). `parent` is null at a root |
| `files.stat` | `path` | an entry, with `path` and `parent`. Error `not_found` |
| `files.read` | `path` or `zip`; `offset` (0), `len` (1 MiB; at most 4 MiB) | `{data_b64, offset, size, mtime_ms, eof}`. A link is followed. Not a file: `invalid` |
| `files.put` | `id, dir, name, offset, data_b64, done?, conflict?, mode?, mtime_ms?`; or `id, dir, name, abort: true` | below |
| `files.mkdir` | `dir, name, conflict?` (`fail`, `keep_both`), `merge?` | `{ok: true, path, name, existed}`, or `{ok: false, code: "exists", message}`. `merge: true`: a folder that is already there is the answer (`existed: true`) |
| `files.create` | `dir, name` | an empty file: `{ok: true, path, name}` or `{ok: false, code: "exists", …}` |
| `files.rename` | `path, name` | in the same folder: `{ok: true, path, name}` or `{ok: false, code: "exists", …}`, or `{ok: false, code: "in_use", message, panes}` for a folder a pane is in. Never in place of anything. A name that differs only in its capitals, on a disk that does not tell them apart, is renamed |
| `files.trash` | `paths` (1 to 1,000) | `{trash, trashed: [path…], failed: [{path, code, message}…]}`. `trash` is the id for Undo, null when nothing was trashed. Codes: `other_disk` (not deleted: ask, then `files.delete`), `in_use` (not deleted, with `panes: [{id, name, project, ended}]`: there is nothing to ask, the panes are closed first), `not_found`, `denied`, `refused`, `invalid`, `failed` |
| `files.restore` | `trash` | `{restored: [path…], failed: [{path, code, message}…], left}`. `exists`: something is there now; that item stays in the trash and can be restored again. `left`: how many are still in the trash. An id that is gone (7 days, or all restored): error `not_found` |
| `files.delete` | `paths` | for good: `{deleted: [path…], failed: [{path, code, message}…]}`. `in_use` as for the trash |
| `files.transfer` | `op` (`copy`, `move`), `paths`, `dir`, `to?`, `conflict?` (`fail`, `replace`, `keep_both`, `skip`) | `{job}`, at once. Below |
| `files.jobs` | | `{jobs: [job…]}`, running ones and those finished in the last 10 minutes |
| `files.job.cancel` | `id` (a job's, or a zip's) | `{ok: true}` |
| `files.zip` | `paths` | `{zip, name}`, at once. Below |
| `files.zip.status` | `id` | `{id, name, state, files_done, files_total, bytes_done, bytes_total, size, code, message, skipped}`. `state`: `building`, `ready`, `failed` |
| `files.ticket` | `path` or `zip`; `station?`, `inline?` | `{ticket, url, expires_ms}`. Below. Asked of the station the browser is on, never forwarded. With `inline` it is kept good by being used (21 Nov 2026, below); `expires_ms` is its first minute |

**An upload** is one `id` (16 to 64 letters, digits, `-` or `_`, made by the page from random bits) and as many `files.put` as it takes, one after another, each with `offset` and at most 4 MiB. Each piece is written to the part file at its offset, and the part then ends where the piece ends. So:

- a piece sent again (its answer was lost) writes the same bytes to the same place: `{ok: true, received}`, where `received` is how much the station now holds;
- a piece that would leave a hole is refused and says what is held: `{ok: false, code: "gap", have, message}`. The page goes on from `have` (0 when the station has nothing, as after 24 idle hours);
- the last piece has `done: true`, and may be empty. The part is given its name by `conflict`: `fail` (the default), `replace` (a file is replaced; a folder never is), `keep_both` (`name (2).ext`, `name (3).ext`, …). The answer is `{ok: true, done: true, path, name, size}`, with the path it really got. When the name is taken under `fail`: `{ok: false, code: "exists", received, message}`, and **the part stays**: the page asks the owner and sends the end again (`offset` = `received`, no data, `done: true`) with `replace` or `keep_both`, or `abort`;
- `mode` (permission bits) and `mtime_ms` are set when given with the last piece;
- a finished upload's answer is kept 10 minutes by its `id`, in memory: the last piece sent again gets the same answer. (After a restart of the station in between, it gets `gap` with `have: 0`, and the file is there under its name: `files.stat` says so.)
- `abort: true` removes the part.

Through another station a piece must make the journey inside that call's time (the page gives a call for another station 6 seconds), so the page sends pieces of 256 KiB there and grows or shrinks them by how long the last one took; to its own station, 1 to 4 MiB.

**A copy or a move** (`files.transfer`) is a job. Without `to` it happens on the station asked: a move is a rename, and across disks, or into a folder that is already there, a copy followed by removing the source, which is removed only if all of it was copied. With `to` (another station's name) it is a copy to `dir` on that station; a move is refused. Folders are walked. Permission bits and modification times are kept. A link to a file is copied as the file; a link to a folder is not followed, and a device or a socket is not taken: each is listed in `skipped` (`link`, `special`). `conflict` is for the items named in `paths`: `fail` (the default) lists the item in `errors` with `exists`; `skip` lists it in `skipped`; `keep_both` makes `name (2)`; `replace` adds to a folder that is there and replaces same-named files in it, and replaces a file with a file (a folder never replaces a file, nor a file a folder). A copy into the folder the item is already in is always a second copy beside it; a folder is never copied into itself (`into_itself`). A move of a folder a pane is in is not made: the item is in `errors` with `in_use` and its `panes`, asked of the panes listed when the job comes to that item, and the rest of the job carries on. A file that fails is listed with its reason and the rest carries on. Cancelling stops between pieces; what was already copied stays.

A job: `{id, op, to, dir, state, counting, files_done, files_total, bytes_done, bytes_total, current, errors, errors_more, skipped, skipped_more, created, message, started_ms, finished_ms}`. `state`: `running`, `done`, `failed`, `cancelled`. `counting` is true while the totals are still being added up. `errors` and `skipped` are `{path, code, message}`, the first 200 of each, with the rest counted. `created`: the top-level paths as they came to be called. `done` with `errors` not empty means "done, except these". `failed` has `message`: the other station did not answer after three tries, or needs updating.

**A zip** of several files and folders is built in a file under the station's state folder and then read like any file (`files.read {zip, offset, len}`, or the download). It is stored, not compressed: what is downloaded is mostly compressed already, and a phone's link is not the slow part. No zip64 is written, so one zip holds at most 20,000 files and folders and 3,584 MB; over that it fails with `too_big` and says what it would have been, and the page offers a copy to a station instead. Names in it are UTF-8 with `/`; times are UTC; permission bits are kept. The file is removed when nothing has read from it for 10 minutes, and at a restart of the station. (Decided against streaming a zip as it is made: its length could not be said beforehand, and a download that breaks could not be told from one that ended.)

**The download**: `GET /api/files/get?path=<absolute path>` on the station the browser is on, with `&station=<name>` for another station's file, `&zip=<id>` in place of `path`, and `&inline=1` for a preview. It is for the owner's signed-in browser, like a font: the browser's token in the `Authorization` header, the same origin check, a pane's token refused (403). A browser saving a file to disk sends no header of ours, so there is a second way in: `files.ticket` gives a `url` (`/api/files/get?ticket=…`) that is good for 60 seconds, for that one file or zip, at that station, from a device under the owner's login (a preview's ticket, `inline`, is kept good by being used: 21 Nov 2026, below). The page navigates to it (or sets it as an `<a download>`'s address); the download itself may then take as long as it takes. The file is read a piece at a time and each piece is sent on before the next is read, so a file of many gigabytes is never held in memory on either station; for another station each piece is one forwarded `files.read`, asked again smaller (down to 64 KiB) when it fails, three times. `Content-Length` is always sent. `Content-Disposition` carries the name twice, plainly and in full (RFC 5987). For `zip` the request waits up to 2 minutes for the zip to be ready (503 `not_ready` after that: ask again), and answers 409 with the zip's own code when it failed. A folder is 400: it is downloaded as a zip. One part of a file is served when a `Range` asks for one (21 Nov 2026, below; before that none were, and a download that broke was started again).

### Where each call stands

- **Asked a second time** (rids.rs). The looks (`files.home`, `files.list`, `files.stat`, `files.read`, `files.jobs`, `files.zip.status`), `files.put` and `files.job.cancel` are safe to send again as they are. `files.put` is never kept by a request id, since a piece is a file's contents: it is safe by its own `id` and `offset`. Done once by a request id, the answer kept: `files.mkdir`, `files.create`, `files.rename`, `files.trash`, `files.restore`, `files.delete`, `files.transfer`, `files.zip` (their answers are paths and ids). `files.ticket` is never kept and never run twice: its answer is a way in.
- **Lanes** (lanes.rs). The looks are reads. Every change is in a line of its own kind, `files`, at its station, this one or another: an upload's pieces keep their order, and hold up neither a pane being started nor any other change there. Typing, Stop, Pause, Kill and an approval's answer wait for no change of any kind, as before.
- **At a keyboard.** `files.jobs`, `files.zip.status`, `files.read` and `files.put` do not count as the owner being at a keyboard: the page asks them by itself for as long as a job or an upload runs, and an upload of an hour must not hold back the push that says a pane needs the owner.

### Move agent here

`pane.restart` takes `cwd`: the pane's program is started again in that folder, under the same pane id and name, as a **new conversation**. An agent's conversation belongs to its folder (Claude Code and Codex both keep sessions by folder), so the old one is not resumed; it stays in the archive. The role is sent again, as to any new conversation. The folder must be an absolute path to a folder that exists; otherwise the call is refused before anything is ended, and the pane runs on as it was. The pane's view shows the new `cwd`, and the ship's log says `moved_from` and `cwd`. Folder trust is the owner's standing choice for the new folder, as for a new pane there. Browsing changes nothing: only this call moves a pane.

What records a pane's folder, and what was done about each:

- **The pane's own record** (`cwd` in what is kept of the pane): written with the new folder. A reload reads it; a restart of the station starts the pane's program again from it (tested: an agent moved, the station killed and started, the agent is back in the new folder and in the conversation it began there).
- **A project's file.** A pane that fills a place in a project is started by `project.open` in the folder the file names, which is the project's folder or one inside it. So **a pane of a project moves inside its project, and the file is given the new folder** (`cwd = "crates/core"` under that pane); **out of its project it is refused**: "… is a pane of the project …, whose folder is …: a project's pane can move to a folder inside its project, not out of it (its place in the project, its orders and the project's record go by that folder). Start a pane in … instead". The reasons are in the sentence: the standing orders are written into the instructions file of the pane's folder, folder trust "for this project" goes by the project's folder, and a project pane working elsewhere would still be answering as the project's.
- **A helper on a task** stays where the task put it (its own worktree, a snapshot, or the tree): its changes and its sign-off are read there. Refused, with a sentence that says so.
- **What an agent made** (the files shown in Chat) is worked out from the pane's folder when it is shown; a new conversation starts with nothing made, so nothing is left pointing at the old folder.

### A project whose folder is gone

A project is a row at its station (a name and a path) and a file in its folder. With none of its panes listed, its folder can be trashed, deleted, renamed or moved like any other, here or by anything else on the machine. What was found, and what was done (the smallest thing that is sound):

- **Nothing broke before, but the words were about a file's name.** `project.list` already answered with the project, `definition: null` and a `problem`; `project.open` was already refused. Both said "… has no .spandeck/project.toml".
- **Now it says what happened and what to do**, in `project.list`'s `problem` and in every refusal that needs the project's file (`project.open`, and whatever else reads it): "the folder of project tally, /…: it was deleted, renamed or moved. Put it back (Undo in Files brings back what was deleted there in the last 7 days), or remove the project from this station". The deck's sidebar already shows a project's `problem` under its name, in the warning colour, and a refused Open is a toast with that sentence; neither was changed.
