- **The row is kept.** The project is not removed from the station when its folder goes: Undo (`files.restore`) puts the folder back with its file, and the project is whole again with no step of its own (tested: it opens, its pane starts in the folder). "Remove from this station" in the project's menu removes the row, as before, and does not need the folder.
- **Not done**: nothing looks for where a renamed or moved folder went, and nothing that is recorded against the old path is changed or removed. A project whose folder was renamed is put right by renaming it back, or by removing the project and adding the folder again.

### The panel

The page side (`crates/web/assets/files.js`, the pictures in `icons.js`, the styles at the end of `app.css`). One panel to a window. On a computer it slides in at the right edge and the panes get narrower beside it: nothing is covered. Its left edge is dragged to size it (300 px at the least, and never so wide that the panes have less than 380 px; the width is kept in this browser). On a phone it is a sheet over the whole screen with a Close button. Escape closes it when it has the keyboard.

**Where it opens.** The folder button and the folder's own words in a pane's strip, the folder line of a conversation's bar (a tap, on a phone), "Files…" in a pane's menu, and "Files on <station>…" in ⌘K. From a pane it opens at that pane's folder on that pane's station and says the pane's name; it shows the station's files, not the pane's. All of these are there only for a station that says `files` in `caps`.

**The rules it keeps.**

- **Browsing never moves a pane.** Only "Move <name> here" ("Restart <name> here" for a shell) calls `pane.restart {pane, cwd}`, and only after a dialog that says what will happen. It is offered when the folder shown is on the pane's station and is not the pane's folder. A refusal is shown in the dialog in the station's own sentence, and the pane runs on.
- **Between stations always a copy.** A drag or a paste onto another station is `files.transfer` with `op: copy` and `to`, asked of the station that has the files. A cut pasted on another station is a copy, and the page says "Copied: between stations files are never moved". On one station a drag moves and with Alt (Option) copies; the badge beside the pointer says which before the drop ("Move 3", "Copy 3 to rtx6000").
- **Names and contents are text.** Every name goes onto the page as a text node, inside `<bdi>` so that a name written right to left cannot reorder the row. A preview of text is a text node in a `<pre>`; nothing a file holds is ever given to the page as HTML. A picture is shown only through the download route with `inline=1`, so only what the station itself finds to be a PNG, JPEG, GIF or WebP is drawn; anything else named `.png` gets "cannot be shown here". A film or a sound is played by the browser's own player through the same route (21 Nov 2026, below), so only what the station itself finds to be one is played; anything else named `.mp4` gets "This cannot be played here. Download it to open it."
- **A name that is not UTF-8** (`odd_name`) is listed greyed, with a note, and cannot be selected, opened, dragged or acted on.
- **A name that is taken is asked about**, never decided silently: Replace, Keep both, Skip. For a copy or a move the destination is listed first and the question is asked once for all the names that clash; the answer is the job's `conflict`. For an upload it is asked when the station answers `exists`, with a tick to do the same for the rest of that upload; the end is then sent again as the protocol says, or the part is aborted.
- **Delete is the trash**, and the toast that says so has **Undo** for ten seconds (`files.restore`). What the station answers `other_disk` for is asked about ("Delete for good? This cannot be undone") and only then `files.delete`.
- **What is in use is not deleted, renamed or moved, and the page never offers to do it anyway.** On `in_use` a dialog names the folder and the panes, as text: "builder and critic (project tally) are working in site or a folder inside it. Close those panes, then delete it." Its only button is OK. When some of what was asked did go, that is said first ("2 items moved to the trash. Not deleted:"), and the toast with Undo for those is there as always. The same for a rename ("then rename it") and for a move this page started, when its job ends ("then move it").
- **The clipboard is the page's own**: a station, paths and copy or cut, shown as a chip ("3 items copied from spark2") with a ✕. It never touches the device's clipboard, and is gone with the page. Files on the device's clipboard are uploaded when pasted with the panel having the keyboard.
- **A station that does not answer is said in its own listing**, with Retry. Looks (`files.home`, `files.list`, `files.jobs`, `files.zip.status`, `files.read` for a preview) are given 10 seconds (6 for a job's or a zip's progress), and at most two are on their way to one station at a time, so the other listing and the rest of the deck go on. An error is the station's sentence, never an object's text.
- **Staying current.** While the panel is open and the page is in sight, the folder shown and each folder open in it are listed again every 4 seconds, one after another; what is selected, open and scrolled to stays. Not while a name or a path is being typed. After each of the page's own changes the listing is read at once, and again when a job that wrote into it ends.
- **A large folder** is drawn 300 rows at a time, more as the owner scrolls; the station's "there were more" is a row that says how many.

**What it has.** A station picker (a station without `files`: listed, greyed, "needs updating"); a path bar whose parts are buttons, and which becomes a box to type a path into (a path that cannot be shown is said under the box and the folder shown stays); Up, Home, the pane's folder, Read again, and "show hidden files" (kept in this browser). One tree: a triangle opens a folder in place, a double-click (a tap, on a touch screen) makes it the folder shown, a file is previewed. A picture for each kind of file, by the end of its name only, drawn in the page from the theme's colours. New folder and New file (named in a row of the tree), Rename in place, Delete, Copy, Cut, Paste, Upload, Download, Copy path, Preview. Click, Ctrl/Cmd-click, Shift-click; the arrow keys, Enter, F2, Delete, Ctrl/Cmd+A, C, X, V.

**Where the actions are** (the owner asked whether right-click and drag and drop make "all those buttons on top" unnecessary; they do, at a computer).

- **At a computer the toolbar is one short row of two buttons**: **New** (a menu: Folder, File) and **⋯**, which opens the same menu as a right-click, for a mouse with no right button. Everything else is in that menu and on the keys.
- **The right-click menu** is for what is selected, or with nothing selected for the folder shown. A file: Preview, Copy, Cut, Rename, Download, Copy path, Delete. A folder: Open, Open in place, the same, and "Paste into <name>". Several: Copy, Cut, Download (one zip), Copy paths, Delete. Empty space: the folder's own. Always under them: New folder, New file, Upload…, Read again; with nothing selected also "Copy this folder's path". **Paste is there only when the page's clipboard holds something.** As in a desktop's file manager, a right-click on a row that is not selected selects it alone first, inside a selection it keeps the selection, and on empty space it selects nothing.
- **A phone, and any touch screen, has no right-click and keeps its buttons**: Upload, New folder, New file, Paste and Select on one line (at 390 px; on a narrower screen they wrap), "Select" for ticks on the rows with what to do with them in a bar at the foot, and each row's "⋯". "Second view": two listings one above the other, each with its own station and folder, to drag from one to the other (not on a phone: there the picker and the clipboard do it).

**Uploads** are the Upload button (a file box, several at once; on an iPhone this is the way in from Files and Photos), a drop of files and folders from the desktop on the listing or on a folder's row (folders are walked with the browser's entries and made with `files.mkdir {merge: true}`), and a paste. Pieces as the protocol says: 1 MiB to the deck's own station growing to 4, 256 KiB through another station growing to 2 MiB or shrinking to 64 KiB by how long the last one took; a piece that fails is sent again from the same place, half as large, four times; `gap` goes on from `have`; then the file is listed as failed and the rest of the batch carries on. One batch at a time.

**Downloads.** One file: `files.ticket`, and the link is handed to the browser and also listed for 55 seconds as "Save" (a phone's browser may not start a download that nobody tapped). Several things or a folder: `files.zip`, a row that shows it being built, then the same. `too_big` is said with the offer to copy to a station instead. **Dragging out to the desktop is one file, in Chrome and Edge only** (`DownloadURL`): a browser allows nothing to be waited for when a drag begins, so the file's ticket is asked for when the pointer has rested on its row for a third of a second and used if it is under 45 seconds old. Folders and several files use Download. In another browser the drag carries nothing out and nothing breaks.

**The foot** lists what is on its way: each upload, each zip, and the jobs of the stations in view (`files.jobs`, asked once a second and only while something runs), as "3 of 12 files · 40 MB of 210 MB · name" with a bar and Cancel; a job done with errors is "done, except 2", which opens to each path and reason; a failed job's `message` is shown as given.

**Decided otherwise than first written down.** The second view is not on a phone. The toolbar at a computer was first two rows of buttons, the selection's always there and greyed; the owner asked for fewer, and it is now New and "⋯" (above), which also never changes height under the pointer. On a touch screen of any width a tap opens and ticks select. The folder button of a strip is not an "icon button" of the strip (the pane's menu stays the strip's first). The nightly run now also runs the station side's two-station test, which no step ran.

**Not built**: a second view on a phone; moving between stations (never, see the rules); dragging several files or a folder out to the desktop; drag and drop on a touch screen; an upload carried on after the page is reloaded; sorting by size or date (the station's order: folders first, then by name); a search.

**Not verified**: a real drag from or to a desktop (the tests hand the page `dragstart`, `dragover` and `drop` events carrying a real `DataTransfer` and real `File`s; a dropped folder is tested with stand-ins for the browser's entries); Safari, Firefox and a real iPhone (only headless Chrome ran, at a phone's width with touch for the phone); the file chooser itself (the test fills the box as the chooser would); `other_disk`, `too_big`, `gap` after a lost piece, a failed job's message and a station that needs updating (written from this document, not seen happen); a download in the app saved on an iPhone's home screen; the deck working through another station (a ticket is asked of the station the page came from); a film or a sound played in Safari or on a real iPhone, and an MP4, a QuickTime film, an MP3, an M4A and an Ogg played anywhere (headless Chrome played a WebM it recorded itself and a WAV; the other kinds were only served and their headers read); a film longer than a second, a ticket that really ran out under a paused film (a link that was never good stood in for it), and the four-hour end of a ticket.

**Tests** (`crates/cli/tests/filespanel.rs`, merge tier, a real browser, real stations, the files read from disk after each step): opened from a pane's strip at the pane's folder, and a part of the path, Up, in place, a typed path, a path that is not there, the arrow keys, Escape, the strip's words and the pane's menu, with the pane never moved; a folder and a file made from New and from the folder's menu, a name that is taken, a rename, the trash and Undo; the toolbar's two buttons, and what the menu holds for a file, a folder, several and empty space, with and without something copied, a right-click selecting a row that was not selected and keeping a selection it is inside; a folder a pane is in not deleted and not renamed, the notice naming the pane with a name that would be markup shown as text and no button but OK, the free item of the same request gone, and the folder deleted once the pane is closed; uploads by the box and by a drop, a file of 3.5 MiB, "Keep both" and "Replace", a dropped folder, a paste; several selected, copied and pasted, cut and pasted; two stations: a selection dragged from one listing to the other and back, the badge, the rows of the foot, a name that is taken, a move and an Alt-copy on one station, a cut pasted across, an upload through the first station in pieces, and the other station going away; markup in a file and in a file's name shown as text with nothing run, a picture and a page named like one, a file that is not text, one cut off, a ticket's link fetched (an attachment, the same bytes), a zip, and the link a drag out carries; Move here on a shell and its refusal for a project's pane; a phone: the sheet, rows a finger can hit, its five buttons on one line, a tap, Select, the row's menu, Close; and beside a layout of two panes (both narrower, neither covered) under Spandeck Dark, Spandeck Light, Solarized Light and Solarized Dark with the owner's own colours, where every kind of text in the panel is measured against what is behind it (4.5:1, and 3:1 for the quiet words), a name that is not UTF-8, a picture for each kind of file, the width dragged and kept, and 5,200 files in one folder.

### Limits, said plainly

- **Not built**: resuming a broken download from the page (parts are served since 21 Nov 2026, so a browser may resume one itself; the page does nothing for it and it was not tried); several parts in one answer (`multipart/byteranges`: the whole file is sent); `If-Range`; an upload's answer surviving a restart of the station; compressing a zip; a zip over 3.5 GB; moving between stations.
- **Not run**: nothing here ran on Windows or macOS. It type-checks for Windows (`scripts/check-windows.sh`). Drive roots, `\` and the hidden bit are written from the documentation, not seen.
- **An older station.** "Needs updating" is said when the other station answers that it has no such method; that was not run against a real older spandeck.
- **Two writers.** `files.rename`, and a top-level `keep_both` or `fail` of a copy, look and then act: something that takes the name in between is replaced by a rename on Unix. An upload's `fail` and `keep_both` do not have this gap (the name is taken by making a link, which fails when it exists), except on a disk that has no links.
- **In use** goes by the folder a pane was started in or moved to. Where a shell has `cd`'d since, and what a program has open, the station does not know: such a folder can be deleted from under it (to the trash, so Undo brings it back). And it looks and then acts: a pane started in a folder in the instant between the look and the rename is not seen.
- **A ticket** is a way in to one file for 60 seconds for anything on the owner's own devices that is shown the link. It is in no log and no kept answer. A preview's ticket (`inline`) lasts longer while it is used: ten minutes from each use, and four hours at the most (21 Nov 2026, below). It still opens one file, as what the station finds it to be.

**Tests** (`crates/core/src/files/tests.rs`, `rpc.rs`, `crates/web/src/files.rs`, `lanes.rs`, `crates/cli/tests/files.rs`). On real folders: a listing's order, bound and what it leaves out; the name check; an upload in pieces with a piece sent twice, a hole, and an abort; the three conflict rules; trash, restore with something in the way, the 7 days, the folders that are never trashed; a folder a pane is in, and the folders above it, not trashed, deleted, renamed or moved while a copy is made, a folder whose name only begins the same let go, the sentence that names the panes, and a link; a tree copied with a link to a file, a link to a folder, modes and times, a file that cannot be written, every conflict rule, a copy beside itself, a move; a cancelled copy; a part file removed after a day across a restart; a zip read back here and by Python's `zipfile` and `unzip` where the machine has them. The lists: no agent, station or read-only caller has a `files.` method; which calls are kept, repeated or never run twice; an upload stands in nobody's way. With real stations: an agent's token and a station's own signed call are refused for every method while the same station carrying the owner's request is answered; the download's headers, an HTML and an SVG file never inline, a PNG inline only when asked, an HTML file named `.png` not; a ticket; a zip through the route; a pane moved, refused out of its project, written into the project's file, and still there after the station is killed and started; trash, delete, rename and move refused with a shell in the folder and in one below it, the free items of the same call done, an ended pane still holding, everything allowed once the pane is closed; a project's folder held by its pane, trashed after `project.close`, the project then listed with what happened to its folder and refused to open in a sentence, and whole again after `files.restore`. Two stations (merge tier): a folder tree copied from one to the other with its modes and every conflict rule, the other way round through the first, a 5 MB file of the other station downloaded through the first, an upload and a zip through it; and, since 21 Nov 2026, parts of a film on one station and through it from the other, each kind of film and sound by its first bytes, a page named `.mp4` and one with a film's header further in, and a ticket's minute waited out.

### A film and a sound play in the preview (21 Nov 2026, the owner's request)

The owner browsed to an `.mp4` an agent had made, mostly from an iPhone. The preview said the file was not text and offered Download; only the four kinds of picture were shown. `crates/web/src/files.rs`, `crates/core/src/files.rs`, `crates/web/assets/files.js`. No method is added, no list changes, no migration, `PROTOCOL` unchanged.

- **The rule is widened by seven kinds and by nothing else.** With `inline=1` a file goes out inline as `video/mp4` (`ftyp` at bytes 4 to 8; MP4 and M4V), `video/quicktime` (the brand `qt  `), `audio/mp4` (the brands `M4A `, `M4B `, `M4P `), `video/webm` (1A 45 DF A3, WebM and Matroska), `audio/mpeg` (`ID3`, or an MPEG audio frame whose version, layer, bit rate and sample rate exist, so that text beginning FF FE is not one), `audio/wav` (`RIFF`…`WAVE`) or `audio/ogg` (`OggS`). A picture kept in an MP4's box (HEIC, AVIF) is not a film and stays an attachment. `nosniff`, the sandbox policy, `Cross-Origin-Resource-Policy` and `no-store` are on every answer as before, and a film's or a sound's type runs no script in a browser. Without `inline` everything is an attachment of no type, as before.
- **By the first bytes, whichever part is asked for.** A part that begins later, or is only a few bytes long (a player's first question is often `bytes=0-1`), costs one more read of 64 bytes at the file's start. A page with a film's header at byte 1,000 is an attachment for every part of it.
- **Parts.** `Range: bytes=a-b`, `bytes=a-` and `bytes=-n` get 206 with `Content-Range` and the length of the part; `Accept-Ranges: bytes` is on every answer. A part the file does not have is 416 with `Content-Range: bytes */<size>`. Several parts at once, another unit and a header that is not understood get the whole file (200), which RFC 9110 allows. A part is read a piece at a time like the whole file, from this station or through it from another, and never held in memory. It holds for a download and a zip too. An iPhone's browser plays no film that is not served this way.
- **A preview's ticket is kept good by being used.** A player asks for part after part over the length of a film, and a ticket was good for a minute. A ticket asked for with `inline` is good for 60 seconds to begin with; each use keeps it good for 10 minutes from then, never beyond 4 hours from when it was given. One that is not `inline` is exactly as before: its minute, used or not. Nothing else about a ticket changes: one file, this station's answer, a device under the owner's login.
- **The page.** By the end of the name (`mp4`, `m4v`, `mov`, `webm`; `mp3`, `m4a`, `wav`, `ogg`) the preview is a `<video controls playsinline preload="metadata">` or an `<audio controls preload="metadata">` with a name a screen reader says, as wide as the dialog and no taller than the screen leaves. The name only chooses the element: the station decides what is sent. When the player fails, a fresh ticket is asked for once and the film goes on from where it was (a ticket that ran out while it was paused for long); a second failure says "This cannot be played here. Download it to open it." Closing the dialog stops the player. Nothing of a file is put on the page as HTML. The preview can be opened by other parts of the page (`previewFile`); Chat does (see "Files an agent sent are shown in Chat").
- **Not built**: a film's size or length before it loads; subtitles; a picture of the film in the listing; other kinds (AVI, FLAC, AAC on its own, MKV with what a browser cannot decode gets the sentence above).

**Tests**: the kinds and what is not one, and which part a `Range` asks for (`crates/web/src/files.rs`); a ticket's sliding (`crates/core/src/files/tests.rs`); real stations and a real browser as listed under Tests above and in `crates/cli/tests/filespanel.rs` (a WebM the browser recorded and a WAV, at a phone's size: the player in the dialog and on the screen, answered 206, a link that is no good asked for again with the film where it was, a stub and a page named `.mp4` said not to play with nothing run).

## A pane's header says more at a glance (19 Nov 2026, the owner's request)

Looking at a conversation's bar (the name, "Claude Code · spark2", the folder): "looks like a place we can add other information. versions, if updates are needed, what else would be good to know at a glance? AgentVault Lock/Key knowing its connected?", and for a terminal: "maybe cpu usage, memory usage?". Shown three drawings of each, the owner chose: **"I like options A with the more graphical chips"**: small rounded chips on a third line of a conversation's bar; the same facts on the one line of a terminal's strip; on a phone a row of two chips and any warning, and a tap for the rest.

- **Always there, when known** (`crates/web/assets/chips.js`, the one place that builds them for both faces): the **model** (the short name the strip always showed); the **context** as "context 62%" with a small gauge, amber from 70 % and red from 90 % as the ring and the strip's gauge already were (with no known window: the count, and no gauge); the **git branch** of the pane's folder with "3 changed" or "clean"; the **estimated cost** of the conversation ("$1.40": the deck's own figure, and its title says "Estimated cost of this conversation at list prices ... An estimate, never a bill"). A shell has no model, context or cost. What is not known is not there: never a made-up number.
- **Only when it is so**, as coloured chips after those:
  - **A newer version of the pane's agent program is published** ("Claude Code 2.1.9 available"; "Update available" where there is less room). **From what the station already holds and nothing else**: the version it last read of the program, and the published one it was last told when the owner asked (Agent programs, "When it is asked"). **A header never makes a station ask a release host or run a program**: that rule stands unchanged. So the chip appears after the owner has opened Agent programs (or run `spandeck agents`) and a newer version was found, and stays until the program is updated; an answer older than three hours is still shown, since nothing here asks again. A click opens Agent programs.
  - **The vault cannot be used**: "Vault locked", "Vault unreachable", "Vault token refused" (red), "Vault not connected", "Vault needs a look" (amber), with a key. From the station's own vault check, which the deck already holds. Nothing when the vault is connected and well, when no vault was found, on a shell's pane (the vault is what an agent's keys come through), or when the station is not connected because the owner answered "Not now" (decided by default: an answer already given is not repeated on every pane; the owner may overrule). A click opens Vault.
  - **Unread mail for the pane** ("2 unread"): messages sent to this pane that it has not read. A click opens the Inbox, as asked. (The Inbox is the owner's; it does not list a pane's own mail. Said plainly: the chip tells, and the click does not yet lead to those messages.)
  - **Held and paused** are shown as they were, where they were.
- **A conversation's bar, at a computer**: the chips are one line under the folder. In a narrow pane the row lets go of the least needed first, each whole: the cost, then how many files are changed, the gauge, the branch, an update's long words for its short ones, the model, the context. **Never a warning**: with nothing left to let go of, the row takes a second line.
- **A terminal's strip**: after the folder, the model, "context 62%" and its gauge, the branch with what is changed, the cost, then any warning; at the right, before the Chat button, **"CPU 34%" and "Mem 1.2 GB" of the pane's own programs**. Still one line. *What differs from "use the existing overflow rules":* the strip's own rule squeezes a piece until it is cut off, and a cost cut off is another cost ("$0.1" for "$0.15" was seen in the first build). So the pieces added here are let go of whole, by the page, before the strip's own rules squeeze anything else: the cost, how many files are changed, the branch, an update's long words, the model, the context, then the processor and memory. State, name, folder and warnings are as before.
- **A phone**: under a conversation's bar one row, 44 px tall, with the model, the context and any warning; with two warnings on a narrow screen the model gives way. A tap opens a sheet that lists everything as a name and its value: model, context, branch, the conversation's cost and tokens, the agent program's version (and the newer one, with the way to update), the vault, mail, the pane's processor and memory, and the station's load. The sheet follows what the station says while it is open. The row is part of the bar, so the conversation scrolls exactly as before, and it goes with the bar when the keyboard is up. A terminal's strip on a phone is as it was.
- **Everything is text.** A branch's name, a model's name, a version and a path are data (an agent can name a branch anything), put on the page as text nodes. Each chip's title and label spell it out: "Context used: 62% of 1,000,000 tokens".
- **Every colour is the look's.** A chip is drawn from the deck's custom properties, so a theme, a layout's look and a pane's own look colour it as they do the bar it is on. In Spandeck Dark a warning is written in its own colour, as drawn; in any other look it is written in the look's text on that colour's shade with that colour's border, so it reads at 4.5:1 whatever was chosen.

### The station's side: `pane.glance`

- **A call of its own, not fields of a pane's view** (`crates/core/src/glance.rs`), for two reasons. The numbers change every few seconds, and a deck's snapshot sends the whole pane list again whenever any pane's view changes. And **an agent reads pane views** (`pane.list`): a branch's name or the load of another project's pane is not an agent's to learn from spandeck, so `pane.glance` is on the read-only list and **on no agent's and no station's**. The agents' list is unchanged.
- **It answers at once from what is held, and works nothing out itself.** A thread of its own does that, every three seconds, **only while somebody asks**: twenty seconds after the last question it stops, and a station nobody looks at samples nothing. The deck asks every four seconds, and only the stations that have a pane on screen.
- **git**: one `git status --porcelain=v2 --branch` per folder (not per pane), kept ten seconds. It is given two seconds and ended after that; a folder that was too slow is left alone for two minutes and says nothing; at most a second and a half of git in a round, so a huge repository delays the next sample and never a request. It takes no lock in the repository (`--no-optional-locks`), so it does not stand in the way of the git an agent runs there. **It runs nothing the repository names**: `core.fsmonitor` in a repository's own settings is a command that `git status` would run, and this call is made without the owner asking, in whatever folder a pane is in, so it is switched off for the call. A folder that is no repository, or a machine without git, says nothing. A detached HEAD says the first seven characters of its commit. "Changed" counts files changed (staged or not) and files git does not know (a folder of them once); ignored files are not counted.
- **Processor and memory** are of everything the pane's program started: every process of its session (a pane's program leads one), and what descends from it by its parents. Processor is the share of **one core** over the time since the last sample, so three busy cores say 300 %; a program that ended between two samples takes its time with it, so the figure can be low for a moment and is never less than nothing. Memory is each program's resident memory added up: what two of them share is counted twice. Platform code is in `crates/terminal/src/sys/` only (`tree_usage`).
  - *Linux*: one read of `/proc/<pid>/stat` for each process on the machine, for all panes together. Measured on the build machine: **3.8 ms a pass with 656 processes, the same for 1 pane or 20**, every three seconds while a deck is open: about a thousandth of one core.
  - *macOS*: libproc (`proc_listallpids`, `proc_pidinfo`), one call a process. **Written without a Mac to compile or run it on; it uses only what the `libc` crate declares.** If it does not compile there, or reads nothing, the two figures are absent and everything else works.
  - *Windows*: **not read; no CPU or Mem there.** A pane's programs are in a job object, whose accounting would say this soundly, but the job belongs to the pane host's process and the station has only the program's id. Asking the host is a change to how the two talk, and was not made here.
- **Mixed versions.** `station.info` says `pane_glance` in `caps`; a deck asks only a station that does. A station from before shows the chips the page can make without it (model, context, cost, the vault). A deck from before never asks.
- **Not built, and said so.** A permission mode other than normal (plan, accept-edits): spandeck does not know a running pane's mode (it knows only what it started a pane with), and nothing is read off the screen for it. What a shell is running, the port it listens on and how its last command ended (the drawings' other options). Graphs of the last minute. A station's disk. The pane's own mail behind the mail chip. Processor and memory on Windows.
- **Tests.** Every change: which processes are a pane's (its session, a program that began a session of its own under it, nothing of the pane beside it), and a process line read whatever the program is called (`sys/unix.rs`); who may call (`rpc.rs`); and with a real station (`crates/cli/tests/chips.rs`, 4 s): the branch and the count for a clean repository, a changed one, a detached HEAD and a folder that is none; a shell that spins using a processor and one that waits using next to none, both holding memory; every agent refused, and nothing of it in a pane's view. Merge tier and nightly, in a real browser: the four chips and their labels in a conversation's bar; a branch named `<img/src=x/onerror=…>` shown as its characters; no warning until one is true, then the update (from an answer the station holds) and the locked vault, each opening its dialog; a narrow pane without the cost and with the warnings whole; the strip's order, its one line, a cost let go of whole; a shell's strip; a layout of two; Spandeck Light and a pane with its own look read at 4.5:1; a phone's row and sheet.

## A station reached over the owner's own SSH (20 Nov 2026, the owner's request)

"If I was on a system without Tailscale, how would I use spandeck and not lose features? In a corp setting, I have my Mac laptop, I have a Linux or docker backend that I can ssh to now ... no admin right either." Asked which fleet such a machine belongs to: "The corp device should never connect to my home fleet, this would be just for the corp system." Asked about the laptop: "We should also support windows." Asked how SSH signs in there: "I have ssh set to use a key." And the condition for building any of it: **nothing that works today may break.**

What is built is the first step of the design the owner read: **a link**. The station on the laptop runs the owner's own `ssh` to the server, and the browser on the laptop opens the server's own Control Deck at a port on `localhost`. This is called deck mode. The two stations do not join. Nothing else of the design is built yet, and the end of this section says what.

- **Nothing changes where there is no link.** A station whose `config.toml` has no `[[link]]` starts no task for links, listens nowhere new, runs no program and answers to no new name. The only new code such a station runs is one look at an empty list when a browser's request arrives. Everything of a link is in `crates/core/src/link/`.
- **The configuration is the only place a link is named.**

  ```toml
  [[link]]
  name = "devbox"                # how it is shown: letters, digits, - _ .
  port = 17400                   # the port on 127.0.0.1 for that station's deck. Left out: one from 17400 up is chosen once and kept
  cmd  = ["ssh", "me@devbox"]    # the carrier: a command whose input and output reach a shell on the far side
  ```

  A link's host and command are read from this file, when the station starts and when the owner at the machine says the file has changed (`link.reload`, which `spandeck link add` and `remove` make after writing it: see the next section), and from nowhere else. **No call adds, changes or removes a link**: not the deck's, not an agent's, not another station's; the one call that reads the file again names nothing and is the local owner's alone. That is what replaces `-F none` of Add a machine, which is unchanged: there, a caller of the deck names the host, so the owner's SSH configuration is kept out; here nobody but the owner at the machine names it, so the owner's configuration is used.
- **The carrier is the owner's own `ssh`, with spandeck's options first.** When `cmd` begins with `ssh` (or `ssh.exe`), spandeck runs it with `-T` and `BatchMode=yes`, `ClearAllForwardings=yes`, `ForwardAgent=no`, `ForwardX11=no`, `PermitLocalCommand=no`, `RemoteCommand=none`, `RequestTTY=no`, `ServerAliveInterval=5`, `ServerAliveCountMax=2`, `StrictHostKeyChecking=yes`, then the owner's own arguments, then the far side's command. The first value given wins in ssh, so these win over the owner's file. Everything else is the owner's: `~/.ssh/config`, `known_hosts`, the bastion, the certificate, the agent. spandeck stores no password, key or ticket, accepts no host key, and forwards nothing. The far side's command is `sh -c 'PATH="$HOME/.local/bin:$PATH"; export PATH; exec spandeck link --stdio'`, so that a `spandeck` that is only in `~/.local/bin` is found by a shell that is not a login shell.
- **Any other command is a carrier too.** `cmd = ["docker", "exec", "-i", "box"]` is run as written with `spandeck link --stdio` added as arguments. The tests use this form, with no SSH at all. The carrier is started without this station's own folders, token or pane in its environment.
- **The far side is a helper, not a listener.** `spandeck link --stdio` is a short-lived program. It speaks the link on its standard input and output and writes what is meant for a person on its standard error. It finds the station on its machine as every `spandeck` command does: the station's private local socket, and the token only that user can read. It opens no port. For each connection the laptop's browser makes, it opens one connection to the station's web listener on `127.0.0.1` and copies bytes both ways. It asks the station where that listener really is, so a port another program has taken is never mistaken for it.
- **If no station runs on the far side, the helper starts one and says so.** The account's login service where there is one (changed the same day: the next section), else a station of its own, detached, so it goes on when the SSH session ends. The helper never installs, updates or replaces anything, and never stops or restarts a station that is running. A far side with no `spandeck`, or one from before links, is said in a sentence with what to do, and is not tried again until the owner says `spandeck link up`.
- **Many streams over one connection** (`link/mux.rs`, our own, a few hundred lines; no new dependency). Eight bytes begin a link, then frames: stream id, kind, flags, length, bytes. A frame is at most 64 KiB and a link carries at most 256 streams. Each end's first frame is a hello with a version number; two versions that differ stop there with "update the older spandeck". **Each stream has its own window** of 256 KiB: an end sends no more than the other has allowed, and allows more only as what arrived is used. So a connection whose browser has stopped reading holds one window and no other stream waits for it, and what is sent is taken a frame from each stream in turn, so megabytes on one delay a key on another by one frame. A link cut in the middle of a frame is told apart from one that ended.
- **Kinds of stream.** `web`: one connection from a browser to the far station's web listener. `ping`: the heartbeat. `sign`: one question about signing a browser in. An end refuses a kind it does not know and nothing else, so kinds can be added; the numbers for a station's call, a pane's socket and a file are kept.
- **The laptop opens streams and takes none.** In deck mode the near side's multiplexer refuses every stream the far end tries to open, whatever its kind, and the link goes on. It runs nothing the far side sends. **What a far side that has been taken over can do to the laptop is serve a hostile page at one `localhost` origin, and nothing else.** Each link has a port of its own, so its page is an origin of its own: it cannot read the laptop station's token or another link's.
- **One port on `127.0.0.1` for each link, and never a hang.** Each connection to it is one `web` stream. While the link is down the browser is answered at once, in plain text: which station is not answering, since when, and that it is being tried again. The page asks again by itself every three seconds.
- **The far station answers to the laptop's address while the helper is attached.** A station refuses a browser whose Host is not one of its own names with its own port. The helper tells it the laptop's port, and it then also answers to `localhost` and `127.0.0.1` with that port, until the helper's connection closes. This is safe against a page that reaches a station by a trick of DNS: such a page arrives with the attacker's host name, never `localhost`. (Found while building: a station serves the deck's page itself to any Host. It is the API and the socket that refuse.)
- **Signing the browser in: `spandeck link open devbox`.** The laptop asks the far station, through the link, for a browser entry. The helper asks its station as the user it runs as. The station makes an ordinary entry, named for the laptop ("macbook (over a link)"), listed under Access and signed out like any other. **The address that is printed carries no token**: `http://localhost:17400/#signin=<id>.<code>`. The code is what the far station hands a token for, once, within two minutes, to a caller on its own machine, which the helper's connection is. The page exchanges it with its own station by the call every browser's sign-in already ends with. So the rule of 15 Nov stands unchanged (a browser's token is never in an address), no token crosses the laptop's station, and the far station's own token never leaves its machine. The laptop keeps the entry's id, in a file only its user can read, and names it the next time: while that entry is still signed in it is used again, and no second one is made.
- **Who may ask.** `link.attach` and `link.signin` (the far side), `link.list`, `link.up`, `link.down` and `link.open` (the near side) are taken only on the station's private local socket and only with the station's own token: not a pane's, and not a signed-in browser's. They are on no agent's list and no station's, and `allowed` refuses them to every caller the dispatcher serves, the owner's browser and the owner's request passed on by another station included (rule 7; `rpc.rs`, `LOCAL_METHODS`).
- **The heartbeat and coming back.** A ping every 2 seconds on a stream of its own; with none answered for 6 seconds the link is "not answering", its carrier is ended and another is started. Trying again: at once, then 100 ms doubling to 5 seconds, each a little more or less. A link that came up and fell straight down is not tried at once again.
- **When SSH wants the owner, spandeck stops and says what to run.** With `BatchMode=yes` ssh fails at once when it would ask for a password, a code or a touch, and when a host's key is unknown or has changed. The link is then "needs you", is tried again only every five minutes, and `spandeck link list` says the command to run in a terminal and then `spandeck link up devbox`.
- **Trying again never changes the far side.** A link that comes back attaches to what is there. Installing and updating are the owner's acts, with the owner present.
- **`spandeck link`.** `add` and `remove`: the next section. `list`: name, port, state, the far station and its version, the round trip, since when, and under a link that is not up, why. `up <name>` and `down <name>`: hold a link closed or open it and try now; kept across a restart; the configuration is not touched. `open <name>`. `--stdio`: the far side. All of it is for the owner at the machine.
- **What the far side says is text.** Its name and version, and what the carrier printed, are cleaned, cut short and shown as text. What goes into the printed address is checked to be an id and a code and nothing else.
- **The ship's log.** Near side: `link.up` and `link.down` with the carrier's command, the far station's name and version, and why it went down; `link.held`, `link.released`, `link.signin`. Far side: `link.attached` and `link.detached` with the port, and the entry made (`trust.approved`, "by you (over a link)").
- **Where there is no Tailscale, that is not a fault.** On a machine with no Tailscale program at all, at a station that has `discovery = false` or has links, `spandeck doctor` says "not installed, and not needed" and the Performance page says the key is not read here. A machine that has Tailscale and is signed out is still told so.
- **Mixed versions.** `station.info` says `link` in `caps`. A far side whose `spandeck` is from before links has no such command; a station there that is older than its own program refuses the helper's first question. Either way the laptop is told, in a sentence, to update it or restart it, and waits for `spandeck link up`.
- **Limits, said plainly.**
  - The far station still has its web listener on `127.0.0.1`, as every station has. On a host other people can log in to, they can reach that port; every call there still needs a token. A station with no TCP listener at all is not built.
  - A browser's token for the far station is kept in the laptop's browser. Whoever can use that browser profile can use that station.
  - On a host that ends a user's processes at logout, a station the helper started ends when the SSH session does.
  - A station the helper starts has the environment of a shell that is not a login shell. An agent there may need proxy or certificate variables that only a login shell sets: `[pane_env]` (the next section).
  - A far side whose default shell has no `sh` (Windows) is not reached by the `ssh` form. A station on Windows can be the near side.
  - The far side's deck shows its own station. Nothing in the laptop's own deck shows links yet: `spandeck link list` does.
- **Not built, and said so.** (Installing spandeck on the far side over SSH was the next step and is built: the next section.) Joining a linked station to a fleet (one deck for both, messages between their agents). The phone. A carrier over HTTPS. A link that closes when nobody is looking. SSH's questions shown in the deck. A control socket of spandeck's own, so that one sign-in serves several connections.
- **Tests.** Every change: the multiplexer as a state machine (a stream nobody reads holds one window and never another stream; more than the window ends the link; a cut in the middle of a frame; every stream the far end opens refused; a frame longer than any may be refused by its header; another version and what is no link at all said plainly; streams past the limit; a finished stream let go), the same with real tasks and a pipe, the options ssh is run with, what ssh's own words become, the waits between tries, and that every caller is refused the calls of a link. Merge tier and nightly, with two real stations and no SSH (`crates/cli/tests/link.rs`): a station with no link listens nowhere new; the far deck's page, a call and the socket work through the port with the entry's token, and a call with none is refused; the printed address holds no token and works once; the entry is listed, used again, and signed out at the far station, which locks the browser out; the far station's token is nowhere at the near one; a frozen far side is "not answering" within the heartbeat's limit and the link comes back by itself, as it does when the carrier is killed; held closed, the browser is told at once; a far side with no station is started; one with no spandeck is said and not hammered; and in a real browser, the printed address opens the far deck with a pane on screen.

### `spandeck link add`: spandeck is put on the far machine over the owner's own ssh (20 Nov 2026, the owner's request)

The second step of the same design. The setting is the one above: a company laptop (a Mac or a Windows PC) and a Linux server the owner reaches with `ssh` and a key. **On the server only the home folder may be assumed**: no root, no system service, no port below 1024, and perhaps no route to GitHub. The condition stands: nothing that works today may break. All of this is additive, and none of it runs unless the owner runs the command or writes `[pane_env]`. This setup is a fleet of its own and never joins or reaches another.

- **`spandeck link add [--name <name>] [--pane-env | --no-pane-env] <ssh's destination and options>`**, run by the owner in a terminal at the machine they sit at: `spandeck link add me@devbox`, `spandeck link add -p 2222 -J me@bastion me@devbox`. The word `ssh`, or the path of one (`C:\Windows\System32\OpenSSH\ssh.exe`), may come first. The name is the host's first label unless `--name` says another. spandeck's own options come before the destination; everything from the destination's first word on is ssh's.
- **Only the destination and ssh's options are taken.** A word after the destination is refused: there ssh takes a command to run, and what runs on the far side is spandeck's to say. Refused too: `-N`, `-f`, `-n`, `-t`, `-s`, `-W`, `-O`, `-G`, `-Q`, `-V`, which make of ssh something a link cannot speak through, and a letter that is no option of ssh. Nothing is run when the arguments are refused.
- **It is the owner's command line and nothing else.** No call of the deck, of an agent or of another station starts it, and it takes the host from its own arguments. The station on the near machine is asked two things, on its private local socket with its own token (`LOCAL_METHODS`): `link.release`, the release for a platform, and `link.reload`, read the `[[link]]`s of the config file again. Neither names a host. Add a machine (`add.rs`), which a caller of the deck starts and which keeps the owner's SSH configuration out with `-F none`, is unchanged.
- **One ssh session, and the owner is at it.** ssh is run with the link's options (`-T`, nothing forwarded, no command of the configuration's, a connection that is noticed when it dies) without `BatchMode` and without `StrictHostKeyChecking=yes`: this once ssh asks what it has to ask, a password, a code, a touch, whether to trust the host's key, and asks the owner, in their terminal. spandeck reads none of it and keeps none of it: no password, key or code is stored, and nothing secret is on a command line. The owner's `~/.ssh/config` and `known_hosts` are used. What ssh itself prints is shown as it prints it. The link that follows runs with `BatchMode` as before.
- **What is looked at on the far side**: its system and processor (`uname`), whether there is a `spandeck` in `~/.local/bin` or on the PATH, its version, and whether it knows `link`. **Nothing is taken from the far machine but the name of its platform**, which must be one of those that have a release and not Windows. Everything else it says is text: cleaned, cut short and printed, with the link's name before it.
- **Where spandeck is missing, the release is sent through that session.** The near machine's station gets it, by the code Add a machine uses (`release_for` in `supply.rs`) and so with the same rules: the published archive for that platform, checked against its published SHA-256; the copy kept from an earlier download; the station's own copy only for a machine of its own kind; never anything older than the near station. It goes into the session's input after the script (`supply::unix_boot_line`, `unix_receive_lines`, `send`), is checked by SHA-256 on arrival, and is installed by the README's install script from that file into `~/.local/bin`. **The far side downloads nothing**: it needs no `curl`, no GitHub and no proxy setting. The owner reads what is sent: "Sending the Linux (x86-64) build of v0.4.20 to devbox over ssh, 18 MB (from GitHub, checked against its published SHA-256)."
- **When there is nothing suitable to send, that is said with the facts and nothing is installed**: "devbox needs the Linux (x86-64) build of spandeck, and this machine cannot get it to send: GitHub does not answer this machine (…), nothing is kept from an earlier download, and this machine is another kind (macOS (Apple silicon)), so its own copy would not run there. Nothing was installed. Run `spandeck link add` again when this machine reaches GitHub, or name a copy of the release page it can reach as `[update] url` in this machine's config." A download that does not match its published checksum is never sent. The near machine's station must be running to get a release; when it is not, that is said before anything is changed.
- **A spandeck that is there is never replaced without a yes.** One that is as new as the near machine's: "nothing is installed or replaced", and nothing is. One that is older: the owner is asked, and the answer is No unless they type yes; on No it stays and the link is added to it. One from before links, which a link cannot reach: asked the same way, and on No nothing is changed on either machine and no link is added. On a yes the release is installed beside the old program and **a station that is running is brought to it in place, with its terminals kept** (`spandeck upgrade`, the live handoff). It is never ended for this.
- **A config file is written only where there is none**: a comment, and `discovery = false` when the machine has no Tailscale program. A config file that is there is not replaced or edited, with the one exception of `[pane_env]` below, on a yes.
- **Start at login on the far side: the first way that works without an administrator, and the owner is told which.**
  1. *The login service* (`spandeck service --install`): on Linux a systemd user service, used only where lingering is on for the user or can be turned on (`loginctl enable-linger`), since without it a user's service stops at logout and does not start at boot. A service that is there already is kept and said as it is, lingering or not. On a Mac, the launchd agent; from an ssh session it may only load at the next login, and that is said.
  2. *One line in the user's crontab*, `@reboot … spandeck daemon # spandeck: start at boot`, where `crontab` works for the user. The line is shown. It is found again by its mark and never written twice. A crontab that cannot be read is never written.
  3. *Started by the link*: nothing is set up. The owner is told what that means: after a reboot the station is down until the near machine connects, and on a host that ends a user's programs at logout it also ends when the last ssh session does.
  A station that runs is not ended or started a second time for any of this: a service is switched on for the next start, and not started beside it. `spandeck doctor` on a station a link has reached says which is in force ("starts when this machine starts, from a line in your crontab", or that a link starts it). No other station runs anything new for that line.
- **A link's helper starts the login service where there is one.** When no station runs on the far side, `spandeck link --stdio` used to start `spandeck daemon` by itself, detached. Where this account's station has a login service (the usual state folder, and the service's file), it now starts that (`systemctl --user start spandeckd`, or `launchctl kickstart`), so the station is one that is kept running; where that does not work, or there is none, it starts the station itself as before.
- **`[pane_env]`**, a table in a station's config: variables set for the process of every pane on that station.

  ```toml
  [pane_env]
  HTTPS_PROXY = "http://proxy.corp.example:3128"
  NODE_EXTRA_CA_CERTS = "/etc/ssl/corp-root.pem"
  ```

  *Why:* a station started by systemd, cron or a link does not have what a login shell sets, and an agent on a company server needs the proxy and the company's certificates to reach its model. With no `[pane_env]` nothing is read and nothing changes for anyone.
  - A name is letters, digits and `_`, at most 64, not beginning with a digit and never `SPANDECK_…`. A value is one line of text, at most 4096 bytes. At most 64 variables. What does not fit is left out, and `spandeck doctor` says which, by name.
  - It comes first: what a pane is started with, and what spandeck itself sets (`PATH`, the pane's token and socket), win over it.
  - It is read when a pane starts, so a change needs no restart and reaches the panes started after it.
  - **A value may be a credential** (a proxy's password). So a value is never logged, never shown, never in an answer of the API and never in the pane's record in the database: the process is given it and nothing keeps it. Only names are said (`spandeck doctor`: "2 variables are set for every pane started from now on: HTTPS_PROXY, NO_PROXY (values are not shown)"). The pane itself has it, as it has its whole environment, and a program in the pane can print it. The values are in the config file, which is the station's own; `link add` leaves the file readable by its user alone when it writes one there.
- **`link add` offers to copy the login shell's proxy and certificates to `[pane_env]`.** It looks, in the far user's login shell, for `HTTPS_PROXY`, `HTTP_PROXY`, `NO_PROXY`, `ALL_PROXY` (and in small letters), `NODE_EXTRA_CA_CERTS`, `SSL_CERT_FILE`, `SSL_CERT_DIR` and `REQUESTS_CA_BUNDLE`. Those that are set and are not in `[pane_env]` are named, **by name only**, and the owner is asked whether to copy them; the answer is No unless they type yes. `--pane-env` and `--no-pane-env` answer in advance. **No value crosses the link or is put on a command line**: the far side's own spandeck is run inside the login shell (`$SHELL -l -c`), reads its own environment, and on a yes writes the table itself (`spandeck link login-env`, not typed by hand). What the shell's own files print is not read for a value. The table is added to under its own line, or as a new table at the end; the rest of the file is byte for byte as it was, and a file that would not read as the same config afterwards is not written.
- **The near side: one `[[link]]` at the end of the config file**, `name` and `cmd` (`["ssh", …the owner's own words…]`). What was in the file is there still, byte for byte, comments and spacing included. A name that is taken is refused before ssh is run; so is a file the entry cannot be added to (one that does not read, or that writes its links as one value), which is then left alone.
- **The station that is running takes the link up without a restart** (`link.reload`): a `[[link]]` that is new is started, one that is gone is ended and its port closed, one whose command or port changed is ended and started as it is now, and one that is as it was is not touched. Then `link add` does what `spandeck link open` does and prints the address. When the near station is not running, that is said, with what to run.
- **Run again, it is how the far side is updated, with the owner present**: `spandeck link add --name devbox`, or the same words as the first time. It looks again, installs nothing where spandeck is new enough, offers the newer release where it is not, offers what the login shell has that `[pane_env]` has not, and opens the link. A name that is taken with another command is refused.
- **At the end the owner is told what now lives in the home folder on the far side** and what removes it: the program (`~/.local/bin/spandeck` and `sd`), the settings (`~/.config/spandeck`), the state (`~/.local/state/spandeck`: history, the archive of sessions, the log), and the service or the crontab's line if one was made.
- **`spandeck link remove <name>`** takes the `[[link]]` out of the near config (its lines and nothing else), has the running station close it, and deletes what was kept for it (its port, and which browser entry was this machine's). **The far side is not touched, and that is said**, with how to take spandeck away there: `spandeck link remove <name> --uninstall` first stops the far station and removes the program, the login service and the crontab's line, over the owner's ssh, with the script the Control Deck's "Uninstall spandeck on …" uses; its settings and its state are kept unless `--erase` is given. (Built because a station has no uninstall command of its own, and the owner must be told a command that removes what `link add` put there.)
- **`spandeck doctor` and HTTPS where there is no Tailscale.** On a machine with no Tailscale program, at a station that has `discovery = false` or has links, the "secure deck (HTTPS)" line is no longer a warning: "not on, and not needed: its certificate would be Tailscale's".
- **Limits, said plainly.**
  - The far side is a Mac or a Linux machine. A far machine whose shell has no `sh` (Windows) is refused in a sentence, with nothing changed. The near side may be Windows: it runs `ssh.exe` and no shell.
  - A release is sent only while the near machine's station runs, since the station is what downloads and checks it.
  - A station started from the crontab or by the link has the PATH of a shell that is not a login shell, with `~/.local/bin` before it. An agent program installed somewhere only a login shell finds is not found; `[pane_env]` does not set the PATH a station looks for programs in.
  - A proxy the station itself would need (for the price list, the check for a newer release) is not taken from `[pane_env]`, which is for panes.
- **Not built, and said so.** `link add` from the Control Deck. A far side on Windows. Updating the far side without the owner at the command line (a release pushed over a link that is up). The switch that stops every call spandeck itself makes to the outside. Joining a linked station to a fleet, the phone, a carrier over HTTPS, and the rest of the list above.
- **Not run.** A real `ssh` and a real `sshd`: every test uses a stand-in, a script named `ssh` that runs what it is given in a shell with a throwaway home folder. So not seen: ssh asking for a password, a code or a host key while its input and output are spandeck's (OpenSSH asks on the terminal, not on its input, which is what this rests on); a bastion; login shells other than `sh` (`bash`, `zsh`, `fish`, `tcsh`) answering `$SHELL -l -c`; a real `crontab`, a real `systemctl --user` and `loginctl enable-linger` (stand-ins); cron's `@reboot` on a real host; a Mac as the far side; a Mac or a Windows PC as the near side (type-checked for Windows, not run).
- **Tests.** Every change: what the owner types becomes the carrier or is refused, and the first ssh is theirs to answer; the `[[link]]` added to the end of a config and taken out again with nothing else moved; `[pane_env]` names checked, values capped and never said, and added to a config with nothing else touched; the calls of a link, the two new ones among them, refused to every caller the dispatcher serves; and the far side's script run by a real `sh` in a pretend home against stand-ins: a config only where there is none, one crontab line never written twice, the user's service only where it outlives a logout, a station that runs not started again. Merge tier and nightly (`crates/cli/tests/linkadd.rs`, two real stations, a stand-in for ssh, a release host): the release sent and installed under the far home, the far station running, the near config with exactly one `[[link]]` more and otherwise byte for byte what it was, the running near station taking it up without a restart, the far deck through the port and the printed address signing a browser in; what ssh was run with, both times; run again, nothing sent or replaced and one crontab line still; `[pane_env]` on a yes, a pane with the variable, and its value in the far config file and in no log, no database, no answer of the API and nothing printed (found while building: a pane's record in the database held its whole environment, so the table is kept out of the record); `link remove`, with and without `--uninstall`; an older spandeck asked about and left alone on No; one from before links left alone with nothing changed; and a far side that needs a release while the near station is down.

## A station is renamed, and no two stations have one name (20 Nov 2026, the owner's request)

- **A station keeps the name it first ran under (25 Nov 2026, the owner's word; before, a station whose config named none asked the machine at every start, and a changed answer was a rename).** *Seen:* the owner's portable, MacBookM5 in macOS's settings, came back from an update as "Mac": a Mac's hostname depends on the network it is on. Mac-mini had become valpomac the same way. A station now writes its name down at every start (`station.name` in its state folder), and a config file with no `station =` line takes the name from there, not from the machine. A `station =` line still decides, so `spandeck station rename` and a rename by hand work as before. **Not covered:** a station already renamed this way keeps the wrong name until the owner renames it back (`spandeck station rename OLD-NAME --station NOW`); taking the line out of a config file no longer goes back to the machine's name.

"We should add the ability to rename a station, now there are 2 RTX6000 listed in stations." What happened: spandeck was put inside a WSL distro on a Windows machine that was already the station `rtx6000`. The distro called itself `RTX6000`, so the new station took that name and joined, and the fleet listed `RTX6000` and `rtx6000`. The same day the owner renamed it by hand (`station = "rtx6000-wsl"` in its config file, and a restart, on 0.4.19): every station then listed both `RTX6000` and `rtx6000-wsl`, both at one address, and "Remove this station" on the old entry would have told the live station at that address to leave and replaced the fleet's key.

A station has no identity but its name, and the name is in a great many places. So a rename is two things, and both are done: **what is stored under the old name is moved to the new one, on every station, once; and the old name goes on finding the station for a year** (`crates/core/src/names.rs`, `rename.rs`).

### Renaming

- **Where.** "Rename…" in the menu of a station that answers, on the fleet screen; `spandeck station rename <new name>` on that station, or with `--station <its present name>` from another. Both are `station.rename`, which is the owner's alone: on no agent's list and no station's. From another station's deck it arrives as the owner's forwarded request, like `station.restart`.
- **The name.** Letters, digits, `-`, `_` and `.`, 64 at most, not beginning with a dot: the rule a name already had to meet wherever it is stored. And not the name of any other station this one lists (a machine that has not joined included), capitals aside, nor a former name of another station that is still remembered. A station's own former name is free to it: that is renaming back. The refusal is one sentence ("Another station is called rtx6000, which differs from Rtx6000 only by capitals: choose a name that cannot be taken for it.").
- **The station keeps running.** The `station =` line of its config file is written (the line is replaced where it stands, a comment after it kept; a file with no such line gets one first; nothing else in the file changes, and a file that would not read back as that name is left alone). Then spandeck there restarts in place, the live handoff an update uses: every pane, agent, conversation and terminal stays open, and a deck reconnects after a moment. **Why a restart and not a change while running:** the name is read at start into every part of the daemon (the relay, the stop book, the approval book, the hosts the web server answers to, what every call to another station is signed as), and a daemon half of which had the new name would be worse than a second's reconnect. If the restart cannot be made the file is put back and nothing changed.
- **The image that starts does the rest.** It sees that its name is not the one it last ran under, and that is the rename. So a rename by hand in the config file is a rename like any other, and so is a machine whose own name changed where the config names none. What it does, before it serves anything: moves what is stored there (below), writes `station.renamed` in the ship's log with who asked, and then, once it has looked at the fleet: saves the synced documents under the new name, tells every member that answers (`fleet.renamed`, a station's signed call only: refused from the owner and from an agent; it can only add to what a former name finds), and raises one notice, which every deck shows: "RTX6000 is now rtx6000-wsl".
- **How the others hear.** From that call, at once. And every station says the renames it knows in its roster (`station.info`: `roster.renamed`, each a former name, the name it became, and when), so a member that was off hears from whichever station it sees first, before it lists anyone. Each station moves what it stores, once, and logs `fleet.renamed`.
- **A former name finds the station for a year** on every station: `x@RTX6000` in an agent's memory, `--station RTX6000`, `peer.call`, a bookmark or a notice's link to `#/s/RTX6000`, a message on its way, a stop's scope, a standing order written for the station. After a year the pair is forgotten and the name is free. A rename is followed to the next, always to a later one, so a second rename and a rename back end the same on every station whatever order they are heard in; a time is later than the clock and than every rename already known, so a slow clock does not put a rename before the one it undoes. Never a name that merely looks alike: see below.

### What names a station, and what is done with each

- **The roster and the list of stations**, on every member: the entry moves to the new name. A former name is never a member: a station that has not heard goes on saying the old name in its roster and is not believed.
- **Pane addresses** (`id@station`, `project/name@station`): both ends of every message kept (`messages.sender`, `messages.recipient`) are rewritten, on the renamed station and on the others, so a pane's unread mail is still its mail, a thread still answers, and the project record still finds it. An address with a former name is read as the station's wherever one is taken (`msg.send`, `pane.get`, an attach).
- **Messages and stops waiting for delivery** (`outbox.dest`, `stops.station`, `stops.scope`): moved, and delivered to the new name by the tasks that already send them. A message that arrives addressed to a former name of this station is this station's.
- **Global layouts and pins** (the deck document) **and looks** (`<station>/<pane>`, `<station>/<project>/<layout>`): read with every station named as it is called now, on every station, at once. And saved so, once, by the renamed station as a change made there, so that every copy is written with the new name, a station from before renames included.
- **A project's layouts** are in the project's own file in the owner's folder. That file is not rewritten for a rename: its leaves are read with the new name, and it says the new name the next time the layout is saved.
- **Links between projects** on different stations (`project_links`), **an agent's approved route to a station** (`routes`), **tasks**: moved.
- **Tools given to a station by name**, and a version held back for one (the tools document): read with the new name, and saved so by the renamed station.
- **Standing orders for a station** (`orders.toml`, `layer = "station"`): an order written for a former name of this station applies to it. A prohibition does not lapse because a station was renamed. The file is the owner's and is not rewritten.
- **Trust.** Requests waiting at a station ("asked at …"), the browsers' "approved at …", and the stations still owed a new key after a removal: moved. The fleet key, browser tokens and pane tokens do not rest on a name and are unchanged.
- **The record of which station's changes a synced document holds** is read with former names resolved, so no change is reported lost for having been made under the old name.
- **What a station said of its Tailscale key**: moved. **HTTPS names are Tailscale's and do not change**: a station's secure address is its machine's name on the tailnet, which spandeck neither sets nor renames.
- **The archive and recall** store no station name: history is labelled when it is asked for, with the name the station has then. Nothing to move, and everything from before is found.
- **In a browser**: which face a pane shows and a phone's fit are kept by `station/pane` and follow the station; the saved list of stations drops the old entry when the fleet stops listing it, as for any station; a link to the old name opens the station.

**Not moved, and said here:** the ship's log and notices already written keep the name that was true when they were written (a notice's link still opens the station). Which parts of the list on the left are folded is kept by station name and is unfolded again for a renamed station. `SPANDECK_STATION` in the environment of a program that was already running keeps the old name until that program is started again; nothing reads it, and an address made from it still arrives. A vault token is the station's and keeps working; at the vault it keeps its old label (`spandeck:<old name>`), which only the vault can change.

### A station listed under a name it no longer has

- **The same station, found by its address.** A member this station lists, at whose address a station now answers under another name, is that station renamed where nobody was told: by hand, on a spandeck from before this, so that it does not know its former name itself. The old entry is replaced and its name becomes a former name. All three must hold: **the same address** (an entry's address is where that station last answered, so the old name answers nowhere else); **the answer was to a call signed with the fleet key, and carried proof**: this station sends a value made for that one question, and the station answers with that value and its own name signed with the fleet's key, which only a holder of the key can make; **and the entry is a member's**, not a machine that never joined. Never on what an unsigned answer says. A station gives that proof only to a station's signed call, never to an agent that reads `station.info`. A station on 0.4.19 or earlier gives none, so it stays listed twice until it is updated; once it is, the others find it, and it is told its own former name by them and says so in the one notice.
- **"Remove" never reaches a station under another name.** Before anything is sent to an entry's address, `fleet.remove` asks who answers there. If a station of this fleet answers under another name (or the name is a former name of a member), nothing is closed and no key is replaced: the name is taken off the list, and the answer says so ("RTX6000 is a former name of rtx6000-wsl, which is in this fleet and was not touched"). If a machine that is not of this fleet answers there, it is sent nothing, and the entry is removed from the fleet's side as an unreachable station is. And the station that is told to leave is told which station is meant: one that is not that station, nor ever was, refuses.

### No two stations have one name, capitals aside

- **A station that asks to join under a name a member has**, capitals aside (or had, and that still finds it), **is not let in under it.** The request says so on every station, in the notice, under Access and in `spandeck trust`. The owner gives the newcomer another name as part of approving, or refuses: the card has a box for the name, filled with a free one (the machine's Tailscale name when that is free, else the name with `-2`); `spandeck approve <code> --as <name>` on the command line. Approving such a request with no name is refused with the sentence and the request goes on waiting. The name is handed over with the key and the station takes it as it joins, by the rename above, before it is listed here under any other.
- **The asker may name itself**: `spandeck fleet --join <station> --as <name>`. It asks under that name and takes it when it is let in, not before.
- **Add a machine** has the same box. It is filled with the machine's Tailscale name when the station would call itself something else (in the incident the Tailscale name, `rtx6000-wsl`, was the better one), or with a free name when a station already has the machine's own. The name is given with the approval, in the same dialog. And a machine is no longer left out of the list for having a station's name: a station found at another machine's Tailscale address is that other machine.
- **A station of its fleet-mates** that comes with a newcomer and has a member's name is said in the request; nothing is renamed for it, and the owner renames one afterwards.
- **A station that comes back** (spandeck put on a machine again, a member of that exact name listed at that address and not answering) is the same station and is let in under its name.
- **A fleet that already has two such names keeps working as it did.** Nothing is renamed on an update, and no station's default name changes. Each of the two is found by its own spelling, exactly; the fleet screen shows one quiet line ("RTX6000 and rtx6000 differ only by capitals. Each is found by its own spelling.") with a button to rename either that answers, and `spandeck fleet` says the same.
- **A name is looked up exactly.** The places that compared names ignoring capitals were three: which station a join asks, which machines Add a machine leaves out as stations, and nothing else that chose a station. A join now takes the name as written, and a name that differs only by capitals only when it could mean one station; so `RTX6000` and `rtx6000` are each asked by their own name and `Rtx6000` finds neither. Everything else (the roster, `--station`, `peer.call`, a message's address, a stop's scope, a layout's leaf) was exact and is: the name as written, else the station that name was a former name of.

### Mixed versions

- A station that knows of renames announces `rename` (`caps`). `[flags] rename = false` makes a station behave as one from before, in both directions.
- **A rename with a member that does not know of renames is allowed, and says so**: the answer, the dialog and the notice name the stations that run a spandeck from before this ("spark1 runs a spandeck from before stations could be renamed: it will list RTX6000 as well, as a station that does not answer, until it is updated") and the stations that are off and did not say, when last seen, that they know ("MacBookM5 is off … if it is still on a spandeck from before this, it will list RTX6000 as well … otherwise it hears of the rename when it is back"). Never silently.
- **What such a station does meanwhile.** It lists both names, the old one as a station that never answers. Its calls reach the renamed station, since the address is the same. A message it holds for the old name is taken by the renamed station as its own. It reads the synced documents as they are written, which is with the new name. What it says of the old name in its roster is not believed by the others. Updated, it finds the station by its address or reads the rename in a roster, and the old entry goes.
- **A station from before that is renamed by hand** is the incident: listed twice by everyone until it is updated, and safe from "Remove" meanwhile at every station that has this.
- **A newcomer from before this** cannot take a name as it is let in. Its request says so; it can be let in under the name it has (the two then differ only by capitals, and the quiet line offers the rename once it is updated) or refused. A request held by a station from before this is not sent a name: the deck says to update that station or approve without a name.

### Add a machine: Tailscale SSH waiting for the owner (the same day)

Adding the WSL machine printed "# Tailscale SSH requires an additional check. # To authenticate, visit: https://login.tailscale.com/a/…" and the dialog said, twelve seconds later, that the machine "did not answer, so it is probably asleep or off".

- The dialog now says what is happening: "Tailscale is waiting for you to confirm this connection", that nothing is wrong with the machine, and the address to confirm at, as a link. **Only an address at `https://login.tailscale.com/` is ever made a link**, cut at the first character such an address does not have, checked by the station and again by the page; everything else ssh printed stays text, as before.
- A connection that ends while that is waited for is made once more and given four minutes, the time a person needs. It goes on by itself when the owner has confirmed.
- Not confirmed in that time, it ends saying so and what to do: "Open the address below, confirm, then Try again: Tailscale remembers it for a while."

### What is not done
